 ix

ABSTRACT
INTRODUCTION AND OBJECTIVES

The objective of this effort is to utilize existing geothermal district heating and cooling (GDHC)
technology, combined with underground thermal energy storage (UTES), to enhance thermal
energy resilience in installations located in cold regions. The goal is to develop a system that
achieves cost savings while supporting resilience for installations in extremely cold climates.

TECHNOLOGY DESCRIPTION

GDHC systems integrate diverse energy loads to optimize overall system efficiency and can be
supplemented with alternative energy sources. Unlike traditional heating and cooling systems,
which operate linearly by delivering hot or cold thermal energy from a producer to a consumer,
GDHC systems enable the reuse of rejected hot or cold temperatures, enhancing energy efficiency.
In cold climates with unbalanced heating and cooling loads, prolonged energy extraction from the
shallow subsurface can lead to a decline in available thermal energy, reducing operational
efficiency over time. Integrating UTES can mitigate this degradation in subsurface energy reserves
and improve the overall efficiency of GDHC systems. While similar configurations have been
successfully demonstrated in temperate climates—for example, the ESTCP-funded UTES at the
Marine Corps Logistics Base in Albany, GA—and individual components have been validated in
cold climates, a comprehensive, holistic demonstration in cold climates has yet to be conducted.
This effort focuses on adapting and optimizing the design, management, and operation of GDHC
systems specifically for heating-dominant cold climates. To demonstrate the efficiency of such a
system in extreme cold temperatures, an integrated simulation demonstration created an optimized
GDHC-UTES system in Fairbanks, AK.

PERFORMANCE AND COST ASSESSMENT

GSHPs in Interior Alaska can be three or more times as efficient as traditional combustion boilers
if sized properly. Several models demonstrate the ideal combination of thermal recharge of the
subsurface that will optimize the installation cost and efficiency of the system. The cost of
installation of a GDHC in Fairbanks, Alaska is high. This is a function of the lack of such systems
in Alaska and the limited workforce. Preliminary cost estimates for the heat pump retrofit
installation (excluding drilling) total approximately $2.85 million, encompassing equipment
purchase, civil work, controls, electrical components, insulation, and general contractor fees.
However, significant cost uncertainties remain regarding drilling expenses (currently quantified as
$2.5 million) and the scope and cost of required contaminant sampling. These elements will require
further investigation and detailed bidding to establish a comprehensive install budget.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
1.0 INTRODUCTION

This report details a joint initiative between the U.S. Army Engineer Research and Development
Center – Cold Regions Research and Engineering Laboratory (ERDC-CRREL) and the National
Laboratory of the Rockies (NLR) to enhance thermal energy resilience in cold regions. The project
focuses on integrating geothermal district heating and cooling technology with underground
thermal energy storage (UTES) through the design and modeling of a borehole thermal energy
storage (BTES) system at Fort Wainwright, Alaska. The final products are 35% and 65% design
development documents that integrate a heat pump system with existing building heating
infrastructure and a borehole field.

2.0 OBJECTIVES

Geothermal energy, harnessed via shallow boreholes and heat pumps, offers a sustainable solution
for heating, cooling, and domestic hot water. Space heating is particularly critical in Arctic and
cold regions. This project aims to demonstrate the feasibility and efficiency of a combined GDHC
and UTES system for military installations in cold regions, bolstering energy security and
resilience in alignment with the Department of Defense’s (DoDs) Arctic Strategy. Key
performance objectives include:

 1. Collecting energy usage data from existing buildings.
 2. Modeling reductions in primary energy for heating and cooling.
 3. Modeling improvements in thermal resilience.
 4. Analyzing system economics.
 5. Securing support from the Ft. Wainwright Directorate of Public Works (DPW).

3.0 TECHNOLOGY DESCRIPTION

Geothermal energy provides solutions for electricity generation and direct and indirect heating,
with system design and depth determined by temperature requirements and regulations. High-
temperature geothermal resources used for electricity generation are typically found deep
underground (beyond 2,500 meters), while shallower subsurface geothermal resources can support
heating and cooling. Arctic nations like the United States, Russia, and Iceland directly utilize
geothermal heat for building heating where naturally warm reservoirs exist; elsewhere, a ground-
source heat pump (GSHP) is used to extract heat for building heating and cooling.

GSHPs leverage the Earth’s stable underground temperatures for efficient heating and cooling,
achieving efficiencies three to five times greater than direct electrical systems (Ahmed et al., 2022)
and representing about 72% of installed geothermal capacity worldwide (Lund and Toth, 2021).
A typical GSHP system generally includes a ground heat exchanger, a heat pump, and a building
heat distribution system. Heat energy is absorbed from the ground and released at higher
temperatures to warm buildings effectively, leveraging the thermal inertia of the ground.

 ES-1

Geothermal district heating systems are most prevalent in Europe, supported by dense populations
and robust policy frameworks encouraging geothermal energy adoption. Geothermal heat pump
systems can offer the added benefit of integrating heating and cooling; they can conserve up to
95% of cooling energy needs by utilizing rejected cold. These systems, often combined with
ground heat exchangers and UTES, deliver enhanced efficiency over individualized systems,
making them a sustainable option for community heating and cooling. For instance, in 2019, a
total of 327 geothermal district and cooling systems were operational across 25 European
countries, highlighting the effectiveness of supportive policies in driving development (European
Geothermal Energy Council, 2020; Soltani et al., 2019).

Thermal energy storage (TES) addresses the challenge of mismatched energy production and
demand by capturing excess energy for later use, reducing resource needs and providing
environmental and economic benefits. Among TES methods, UTES stores substantial energy in
subsurface environments and is particularly suitable for regions requiring seasonal heating or
cooling. UTES technologies include boreholes, aquifers, caverns, pits, or tanks, with applications
varying based on local conditions and needs.

BTES systems use closed fluid-filled piping in boreholes, avoiding interaction with natural
groundwater and allowing for the use of antifreeze fluids. These systems typically involve vertical
boreholes, connected to heat pumps, and rely on the ground's thermal properties for energy
transfer. While BTES systems are versatile and effective, they can be costly due to the need to drill
many boreholes.

Globally, geothermal heating and cooling reached 107,727 megawatts thermal (MWt) by the end
of 2019, a 52% increase since 2015 (Lund and Toth, 2021), with GHSPs accounting for 72% of
this capacity. Leading countries include China, the United States, Sweden, Germany, and Finland,
while Iceland, Sweden, Finland, Switzerland, and Norway lead in per capita capacity.

Case studies demonstrate successful implementation in diverse climates. A BTES at a Marine
Corps base in Albany, Georgia, reduced heating, ventilation, and air conditioning (HVAC) energy
consumption by 47.5% (Hammock and Sullens, 2017). Canada’s Drake Landing system supports
52 homes with solar-heated fluid (Canmet Energy, 2009). Sweden and Finland utilize BTES for
waste heat storage and demonstrate efficiencies up to 60% in cold regions (Skarphagen et al.,
2019). In Alaska, GSHP systems are mainly found in non-Arctic regions due to challenges posed
by permafrost, though innovative projects, such as one integrating solar thermal recharge, have
demonstrated potential in subarctic areas.

4.0 PERFORMANCE ASSESSMENT

The project demonstration was conducted at Fort Wainwright in Fairbanks, Alaska (Figure ES 1),
a region characterized by a continental sub-Arctic climate with a mean annual air temperature of -
2.1°C and discontinuous permafrost on the cusp of thawing. While the installation's existing
district heating system supplies most buildings with high-temperature hydronic heat (71-82°C),

 ES-2

two newer facilities, Buildings 3001 and 3002, are ideal for this demonstration as they already
utilize in-floor radiant heat and are situated near areas with potential for a BTES system.

 Figure ES 1. Fairbanks (west), and Fort Wainwright (east). The location of selected
buildings used in this study. Location of Fairbanks is shown in the inset of the upper right
corner. (Powered by Esri, HERE, Garmin, Intermap, increment P Corp., GEBCO, USGS,
 FAO, NPS, NRCAN, GeoBase, IGN, Kadaster NL, Ordnance Survey, Esri China (Hong
 Kong), (c) OpenStreetMap contributors, and the GIS User Community.)

Because the high delivery temperatures of the main district heating system are inefficient for heat
pump operation, this project focused on these two newer facilities—Building 3001 (a 1,393 m²
company operations facility) and Building 3002 (an 1,895 m² Battalion Headquarters) (Figure ES
2). Constructed in 2016, both buildings feature modern, low-temperature in-floor hydronic
systems, making them ideal candidates for a heat pump retrofit. Their location is also
advantageous, as they are situated adjacent to a large green space suitable for the installation of
the necessary borehole field.

 ES-3

 a)

 b)

 c) d)

 Figure ES 2. Building 3001 thermal zones for the a) lower, and b) upper levels, and
 Building 3002 thermal zones for the c) lower, and d) upper levels.

 ES-4

Accurate modeling is crucial for optimizing GSHP and BTES system design. The process begins
by defining the building’s specific heating and cooling needs using building energy simulation
software like EnergyPlus. This foundational load data is then used by more specialized geothermal
models to predict subsurface temperatures and design the BTES configuration to meet seasonal
and peak demands. Analytical models (e.g. GLHEPro) provide efficient long-term performance
estimates, while numerical models (e.g. COMSOL) can offer more detailed simulations of
complex subsurface interactions.

The project design was validated through a comprehensive, multi-stage modeling workflow that
addressed everything from initial loads to long-term environmental impact. The process
systematically integrated several tools: it began with EnergyPlus to define building energy loads,
followed by GLHEPro for initial borehole field sizing and a Superposition Borehole Method for
more detailed design. Finally, GeoStudio was employed to conduct a 25-year simulation of the
subsurface environmental effects, a role for which the more intensive COMSOL Multiphysics was
also evaluated. This rigorous, step-by-step approach ensured the proposed system is both efficient
and resilient.

The results of the comprehensive EnergyPlus analysis project substantial energy savings from the
proposed geothermal heat pump installation. As shown in Figure ES 3, which is based on the loads
calculated by EnergyPlus, the models indicate a significant 36% annual heating energy reduction
for Building 3002 and a 55% reduction for Building 3001. These impressive savings are a direct
result of the system's high efficiency, achieving a coefficient of performance (CoP) of 3. In the
figure, the 'pre' value represents the current heat load, while 'post' is the projected load after
conversion to GDHC. It is important to note that this analysis focuses specifically on the building
zones compatible with low-temperature heat delivery.

  Figure ES 3. Annual building and zone heating for current load (“Pre” installation) and
 after the heat pump installation (“Post” installation) for Buildings 3001 and 3002.

 ES-5

 Modeling and analysis were conducted in this work to determine the optimal system configuration
 for a GDHC with BTES system at Fort Wainwright, Alaska. Multiple computer simulations were
 developed to mesh the building energy requirements with the potential heat energy available in the
 ground. Designing a sustainable GDHC and BTES system in a location with a much larger heating
 than cooling load requires injecting additional heat into the BTES system. The design of a BTES
 system was simulated in two phases to determine the optimal configuration for two different land
 plots (Table ES 1). The first phase (cases 1-3) involved injecting additional heat during the summer
 of only the first 5 years of operation of the GSHP system. The second phase (cases 4-6) involved
 continuous heat injection every summer for the entire lifespan of the system.

 Table ES 1. Design cases and resulting borehole configurations from the analytical
 modeling.

  5         1 or 2          2 x 22            5             44           94         4,136         -9.1, 96.3
  6         1 or 2          2 x 20            5             40           94         3,760         -9.1, 95.6

 Injecting heat only during the initial 5 years required a substantially larger number of boreholes
 and very high temperatures in the ground loop in summer when attempting to cool the buildings.
 Adding supplemental heat throughout the lifetime of the GDHC and BTES system provided a
 much more sustainable design and reasonable ground loop temperatures. Furthermore, adding
 supplemental heat every year considerably lowered the rate at which supplemental heat would be
 required. Increasing the amount of supplemental heat added to the system decreased the number
 of boreholes required. The borehole thermal load and entering and leaving fluid temperature for
 the most balanced system (design case 4), is illustrated in Figure ES 4. Modeling showed that a
 GDHC with BTES system can be designed to meet the building loads while fitting the BTES
 within the smaller rectangular space of Location 1 (220 meters by 40 meters) or the larger area of
 Location 2 (250 meters by 200 meters).

 ES-6

 Figure ES 4. Borehole thermal loads for design case 4 (top), and BTES entering (left,
 bottom) and leaving (right, bottom) fluid temperatures.

Subsurface model results give insight into the location and magnitude of heat stored in the
subsurface around the BTES. As the boreholes are used to collect and reject heat to the subsurface
to provide building heating and cooling, the surrounding groundwater heats and cools. No
significant effect on surrounding groundwater temperatures is observed when heating and cooling
loads are balanced such as in design case 4. In simulations involving injecting large amounts of
heat such as design cases 5 and 6, water warmer than natural groundwater drifts downstream from
the BTES over time with the natural flow of groundwater. After 25 years of use of the BTES with
the highest heat injection rate modeled for this study, three times the yearly net heating demand of
the buildings, elevated groundwater temperature by 1°C can be observed 579m downstream of the
boreholes in the model. Groundwater closer to the BTES has higher temperatures due to the heat
injected, reaching 20°C at 50m downstream of the boreholes in this high heat addition case (design
case 6) after 15 years. This thermal plume could have significant downstream impacts. If it
interacts with surface water, the warmer temperatures can reduce dissolved oxygen, harming fish
populations, and promote harmful algal blooms.

In regions with permafrost, the consequences are even more severe. The introduction of warm
groundwater can thaw the frozen soil, leading to ground subsidence and destabilizing critical
infrastructure such as building foundations, roads, and pipelines if built on permafrost. Therefore,
understanding the path and temperature of this plume is critical for long-term environmental and
geotechnical risk management if these high supplemental heat rates are pursued.

 ES-7

5.0 COST ASSESSMENT

The design of the installation of the proposed system is fully documented through detailed
schematic drawings and discipline narratives (Appendices B-1, C-1, and D-1), making it ready for
contractor review and cost estimation. Preliminary cost estimates for installation total
approximately $2.85 million for civil, electrical, controls, and general construction, with drilling
costs estimated at $2.5 million. However, it is critical to note these figures are based on a single
contractor bid, as our multiple requests for proposals yielded only one response. This limited our
ability to perform a comparative analysis or secure a granular breakdown. Consequently, the final
project budget remains contingent upon verifying these costs through competitive bidding and
completing contaminant sampling assessments, which represent the key areas of ongoing financial
uncertainty.

6.0 IMPLEMENTATION ISSUES

Results indicated that design case 4, utilizing a balanced system with 63 boreholes each 85m deep,
offered the preferable performance. In the balanced system design, the energy extracted from the
ground heat exchanger (GHX) is replaced with the same amount of energy, using heat from the
combined heat and power plant (CHP) over the life the system. Adding less energy lowered the
performance of the system over time and adding excess energy had the potential to thaw permafrost
downstream of the GHX. The proposed system centers around an 80-ton water-to-water heat pump
utilizing a 50% glycol solution in the ground heat exchanger that is capable of delivering a 54°C
discharge temperature with an expected source temperature of 4°C. A single circulating pump will
distribute heated fluid to Buildings 3001 and 3002, while redundant duplex pumps ensure
continuous ground loop circulation.

Implementation of geothermal systems, while offering significant long-term energy efficiency
benefits, presents several potential challenges. Subsurface geological conditions are critical;
heterogeneous soil or rock layers can impact thermal conductivity and storage capacity, requiring
extensive site characterization. Drilling operations can be costly and may encounter unforeseen
subsurface obstacles. Long-term system performance relies on maintaining borehole integrity, as
grout degradation or groundwater flow can compromise thermal efficiency. Furthermore,
integration with existing HVAC systems requires careful engineering to ensure compatibility and
optimal operational sequencing. Finally, permitting and regulatory hurdles related to geothermal
installations and potential groundwater impacts must be addressed, potentially extending project
timelines and increasing costs. Thorough planning, detailed site investigations, and robust system
monitoring are essential to mitigate these implementation risks.

This design provides a solid foundation for construction and realizing the potential energy
efficiency gains of a GDHC with BTES system in a sub-Arctic environment. The completed 35%
and 65% design packages will enable accurate cost estimates and facilitate the next phase of project
– implementation that will contribute to a more sustainable and resilient energy future for Fort
Wainwright, and other cold region installations.

 ES-8

1.0 INTRODUCTION
This project is part of a collaborative effort between the U.S. Army Engineer Research and
Development Center (ERDC) - Cold Regions Research and Engineering Laboratory (CRREL) and
the National Laboratory of the Rockies (NLR). The goal is to leverage geothermal district heating
and cooling (GDHC) technology, in combination with underground thermal energy storage
(UTES), to enhance thermal energy resilience in cold-region installations. This report will review
recent publications on the system design and technology used in geothermal energy systems,
including advancements in technology, design and modeling techniques, as well as case studies.
The aim is to understand how these findings and case studies can inform the design and modeling
of a cold-climate borehole thermal energy storage (BTES) system in Fairbanks, Alaska.
Additionally, these findings will help assess the feasibility of implementing a GDHC in a cold
climate.

1.1 BACKGROUND

Geothermal energy is the heat stored in the Earth’s subsurface, offering a promising solution to
meet energy demands. This energy is often harnessed from natural hydrothermal resources such
as steam or hot water accessed through deep wells. Shallow boreholes can also be used to tap into
the Earth’s energy without extracting water, employing GSHPs to take advantage of near constant
underground temperatures for heating or cooling as needed. These shallow geothermal systems
typically do not extend deeper than 400 meters below the surface (Tsagarakis et al., 2020). Such
systems have been utilized since the early 19th century (Soltani et al., 2019) and have continued
to advance. Building systems can leverage this temperature exchange to heat buildings in the
winter, cool them in the summer, and provide domestic hot water.

The Arctic region, consisting of the eight nations that compose the Arctic Council—Canada, the
Kingdom of Denmark (including Greenland), Finland, Iceland, Norway, Russia, Sweden, and the
United States (Lee, 2019) —faces unique energy challenges. In the Arctic, heating demands far
exceed cooling needs, making it a heating-dominated climate. Geothermal energy offers a
promising solution to address these challenges. Small, remote communities in Arctic countries are
often disconnected from large energy grids. These communities rely on local energy resources,
most of which (about 80%) come from fossil fuels such as diesel, natural gas, and coal (De Witt
et al., 2019). Unfortunately, many of these communities lack reliable backup systems. In these
harsh climates, energy loss can be life-threatening (Wiltse et al., 2014). To mitigate this risk, Arctic
communities must stay vigilant about potential fuel supply disruptions and find ways to improve
resilience while keeping costs low. Due to the extreme climate, about 70% of energy use is
dedicated to heating.

Large-scale examples of GDHC systems, UTES systems, and GSHPs are well established in cold
climates individually. However, research is still needed to understand the feasibility, functionality,
and reliability of combining these systems in cold climate settings.

 1

1.2 OBJECTIVE OF THE DEMONSTRATION

Focusing on designing a system for Fort Wainwright, Alaska provides an opportunity to
demonstrate the feasibility of GDHC with UTES in extreme cold regions. Located in Interior
Alaska in Fairbanks, this location experiences extremely cold temperatures, reaching as low as -
40°C. The city has 7,407 base 18°C heating degree days (13,366 HDD65F) (ASHRAE, 2021). With
fewer than 100 cooling degree days, Fairbanks has a heating-dominated climate. Space heating
accounts for the majority of building energy use in Fairbanks, comprising 79% of total energy
consumption (Figure 1-1).

 Figure 1-1. Fairbanks building energy use (Carlson and Zhang, 2015) and energy
 characteristics (Madden, 2018).

The objective of this demonstration is to estimate the efficiency of a GDHC system for building
loads typical of military installations in extreme cold climates with high heating demands. The
models will help assess the feasibility of implementing a district geothermal heat pump system in
a heating-dominated climate. Additionally, the project will support the evaluation of potential
energy savings from using a district geothermal heat pump system compared to coal-based district
heating. Finally, the project will offer design recommendations for retrofitting district geothermal
heat pump systems into cold-climate buildings.

1.3 REGULATORY DRIVERS

In 2022 DoD established the Arctic Strategy and Global Resilience Office. That same year, the
White House released the National Strategy for the Arctic Region, which outlines four key pillars.
One of these pillars (Pillar 2) focuses on building resilience (The White House, 2022). The use of
geothermal technology for heating, cooling, and energy storage at cold-region military installations
can significantly enhance their resilience and reduce fuel costs. A GDHC system consisting of
GSHP combined with UTES at Fort Wainwright, Alaska, serves as a prime example of how the
DoD can increase resilience in heating systems at Arctic installations.
 2

2.0 TECHNOLOGY DESCRIPTION
2.1 GEOTHERMAL HEATING AND COOLING

Geothermal energy can be harnessed for various applications, including electricity generation and
direct use of heat. A variety of factors can dictate the optimal depth of a system design, such as
desired temperatures or required permits for particular drilling depths (Goetzl, 2022). Generally,
higher temperatures are found deeper underground. High-temperature geothermal fluid is
commonly utilized in wellbores with a depth of greater than 2,500 meters (Budiono et al., 2022).
Heating and cooling from the Earth’s shallow subsurface is possible where the soil temperatures
are both above and below freezing (Eslami-nejad and Bernier, 2012; Garber-Slaght and Peterson,
2017).

2.1.1 Direct Use

Geothermal heat is directly used for building heating in many regions, including Arctic nations.
Kolker et al. (2022) examines the technologies and feasibility of geothermal energy systems in
Arctic countries. The United States, Russia, and Iceland all have geothermal direct-use
applications, including district heating systems. However, most of these direct-use applications are
not located in the Arctic; they are typically found in areas with warm geothermal reservoirs near
the surface. In regions where shallow, warm reservoirs are unavailable, GSHPs can be used to
transfer low-temperature heat from the ground to heat buildings.

2.1.2 Ground-Source Heat Pump (GSHP)

GSHPs, also known as geothermal heat pumps (GHPs), use the Earth's constant subsurface
temperature to exchange heat as needed through a ground heat exchanger (GHX). Heat pumps can
also use ambient air to exchange heat (air-source heat pumps). In cold climates, air temperatures
can be significantly colder than ground temperatures, resulting in less energy available for heating
from the air. One advantage of GSHPs is that they leverage the relatively high thermal inertia or
heat capacity of the ground. The subsurface is generally cooler than the air in the summer and
warmer than the air in the winter, allowing for the extraction and/or storage of heat in the ground.
GSHPs have high efficiencies of 300% to 500% for ratios of heat provided for electricity used,
compared to an electric heaters where the efficiency would be only 100% (Ahmed et al., 2022).
GSHP systems are the most widely used form of geothermal energy worldwide, accounting for
about 72% of installed capacity (Lund and Toth, 2021).

Closed-loop GSHP systems consist of three main components: a ground loop (the GHX), a heat
pump appliance, and a building heat distribution system (Figure 2-1). Thermal energy from the
subsurface is transferred to a circulating thermal fluid, which then flows to the heat pump at the
surface (Geothermal Technologies Office, 2023). The ground loop can be a horizontal system of
pipes buried in a trench or a pond. Alternatively, the loop can be a vertical system of pipes placed
in boreholes, known as a borehole heat exchanger (BHE). In open-loop systems, groundwater is
used directly instead of a closed ground loop.

 3

The heat pump appliance operates a refrigeration cycle. It absorbs energy from the fluid circulating
through the subsurface and uses that energy to evaporate a refrigerant. The refrigerant gas is then
compressed using electricity. As the gas condenses, it releases higher-temperature energy, which
is used to heat the building.

 Figure 2-1. Mechanics of a heat pump (from Garber-Slaght and Stevens (2014)).

A district heating system centralizes the production and distribution of heat, making it more
efficient for heating multiple buildings in close proximity. In cold climates, higher heating loads
make the installation and operation of district heating systems more advantageous, as they provide
greater efficiency and profitability. These systems account for 70% of direct-use geothermal
systems in Iceland, 19% in the United States, and 26% in Russia (Kolker et al., 2022). In Nordic
countries, district heating systems are commonly used to supply hot water and heating to urban
homes (Lund and Toth, 2021). However, many of these systems rely on steam or higher-
temperature fluid that cannot be easily provided by GSHPs. It is important to note that most heat
pumps cannot be retrofitted into these existing systems without a system overhaul due to the high
building side delivery temperatures.

Geothermal heating systems are most prevalent in Europe, with 2 million heat pumps installed in
2019 (European Geothermal Energy Council, 2020; Soltani et al., 2019). Europe’s relatively high
population density creates both a demand and a technical framework that makes geothermal district
heating systems particularly attractive. The European Geothermal Energy Council credits Europe’s
policy framework as being "crucial in supporting [geothermal] development," with the
organization’s Secretary General, Philippe Dumas, urging European and national policies that
“encourage this trend” (European Geothermal Energy Council, 2020).

 4

Using a district system for both heating and cooling allows for the movement of rejected hot or
cold fluids around the system to where they are needed, minimizing lost energy. GDHC can
function as a network of distributed generation, such as GHX and UTES, integrated into
community infrastructure. These systems are designed to provide more efficient heating and
cooling than individual, standalone systems (Figure 2-2).

Figure 2-2. Illustration of a geothermal district heating and cooling (GDHC) system which
 uses GSHPs (from Department of Energy’s public domain, DOE (2023))

2.2 UNDERGROUND THERMAL ENERGY SYSTEM (UTES)

Optimizing the timing of energy production can be challenging, as the amount of energy produced
often does not match demand, either being insufficient or excessive. Thermal energy storage (TES)
helps bridge this gap by capturing excess energy and storing it for later use. By storing unused
heat, TES systems reduce the need for additional resources to meet energy demands, providing
both environmental and economic benefits. Excess energy from industrial processes and
intermittent renewable sources can be stored for later use. TES systems vary widely in design and
application, with many large-scale, viable examples implemented worldwide (Alva et al., 2018;
Xu et al., 2021).

UTES store large amounts of energy in the subsurface. While less common than smaller-scale
GHX systems, UTES is still widely used around the world (Zody and Gisladottir, 2023). These
systems are particularly suited for regions with a high demand for seasonal TES (Alva et al., 2018).
UTES is often used for either heating (Alva et al., 2018) or cooling (Geothermal Technologies
Office 2023) on a seasonal basis. UTES systems have been demonstrated in various forms,
including boreholes, aquifers, caverns, pits, and tanks (Alva et al., 2018).

 5

2.2.1 Aquifer Thermal Energy Storage (ATES)

Aquifers are naturally occurring underground reservoirs of water found in the pore space within
rock or soil. Aquifer thermal energy storage (ATES) involves using a well to access groundwater
within an aquifer for storage thermal energy. ATES is only feasible where there is an adequate
supply of groundwater, and where local groundwater regulations permit the extraction and release
of water into wells. After use, the water can be reinjected into the same or a different underground
aquifer. Systems can be designed with separate wells for extraction and reinjection, or with a single
well serving both functions, as is the case with standing column wells.

To effectively store thermal energy in aquifers, certain conditions must be met. The aquifer should
be confined, with minimal natural groundwater flow in or out, and must have a hydraulic
conductivity greater than 1.1 m/s (Xu et al., 2013). Ideally, the thickness of the aquifer should
range from 20 to 50 meters.

ATES systems have the advantage of using groundwater directly, but they can also face challenges
such as sediment, chemical deposition, or biological buildup. The heat in the groundwater is used
directly, rather than transferring the heat to another fluid before use such as in BTES, which means
fewer wells are required for ATES compared to BTES. As a result, ATES systems often require
less surface land area, making them a potentially more attractive option for well-developed areas
(Soltani et al., 2019). However, the requirement for sufficiently permeable aquifers accessible with
drilled wellscan be challenging in some regions. While ATES generally supports larger thermal
loads than BTES (Pellegrini et al., 2019), regulations may complicate its use, as the water after
used for heating or cooling may be classified as wastewater in some cases.

2.2.2 Borehole Thermal Energy Storage (BTES)

BTES systems use a closed loop of fluid within boreholes to transfer heat, ensuring that natural
groundwater is not directly accessed. Typically, high-density polyethylene (HDPE) U-bend pipes
are inserted into boreholes and grouted into place to form a borehole heat exchanger often called
a ground loop. This setup minimizes the risk of contaminating groundwater and allows for the use
of antifreeze in the ground loop. The ground loops used in BTES systems are similar to those in
GSHP systems (Soltani et al., 2019). The primary distinction between BHEs and BTES systems is
the incorporation of energy storage in BTES, as both use BHEs.

BTES systems typically consist of multiple vertical boreholes. In the U.S., boreholes are generally
30-120 meters deep, with heat exchanger piping installed inside (Geothermal Technologies Office,
2023). In Europe, boreholes as deep as 500 meters are common. The space between the borehole
and the soil is often filled with high-thermal-conductivity grout or groundwater. Insulation is
sometimes applied at the surface to minimize heat loss. The vertical pipes in the boreholes are
connected by horizontal piping placed in a trench and linked to a heat pump (Geothermal
Technologies Office, 2023).

 6

For BTES systems to function effectively, the soil must have a high volumetric heat capacity, as
these systems rely on the ground’s ability to store heat (Ghelin, 2016). The presence of
groundwater is preferred because water has a high specific heat capacity. However, groundwater
flow can significantly impact system performance and must be considered when designing a
system (Xu et al., 2013). Groundwater flow has been shown to have positive effects on system
efficiency and longevity in a single-well BHE, but negatively impact BTES efficiency for
sufficiently high subsurface flow velocities (Brown et al., 2023). Vertical borehole heat exchangers
make BTES systems one of the highest specific-cost in UTES systems.

2.3 GROUND LOOP SYSTEM DESIGN

A ground loop, or GHX, is a series of underground pipes that serve as a critical link for GSHPs to
exchange heat with the ground. These systems can be arranged in parallel, in series, or as a
combination of the two. The ideal design depends on the land and cost constraints of the specific
application.

There are two main types of piping used in closed-loop BHE systems: U-shape and coaxial (i.e.,
pipe-in-pipe). In a U-shape design, a U-shaped pipe is lowered into a borehole, routing the fluid
down into the borehole and then back up. Coaxial designs, while effective, require more time and
effort to install and are therefore less common in GSHP systems.

The placement and design of boreholes in BTES systems are crucial to minimize thermal
interference between individual boreholes, ensuring efficient thermal storage. Boreholes are
typically spaced 5-15 meters apart (Diersch and Bauer, 2015). According to Ghelin (2016), a
design with a small surface-to-volume ratio is preferable for maximizing storage capacity while
minimizing heat loss through the surface area of the storage volume. If surface area is a limiting
factor, the system does not need to be linear; it can spread out in a "broom-like shape" if deviated
drilling is feasible (Ghelin, 2016). From a heat loss perspective, a sphere would be the most ideal
shape for storage in an infinite medium. However, due to construction constraints, cylindrical and
box-shaped arrangements are more commonly used. Symmetrical configurations are typically
employed (Ghelin, 2016).

2.4 CURRENT STATE OF GEOTHERMAL HEATING AND COOLING

Geothermal heating and cooling systems are installed worldwide. By the end of 2019, the
estimated installed thermal power was 107,727MWt, marking a 52.0% increase from 2015 (Lund
and Toth, 2021). In 2019, GSHP systems accounted for 71.6% of the total thermal power capacity
and 58.8% of the energy usage (Lund and Toth, 2021). China, the United States, Sweden,
Germany, and Finland lead the world in installed GSHP capacity. However, when considering
land area and population, Iceland, Sweden, Finland, Switzerland, and Norway are the top five
countries demonstrating the effective use of geothermal heating and cooling in cold regions (Lund
and Toth, 2021).

 7

In 2015, a BTES system was installed at Marine Corps Logistics base in Albany, GA, USA, to
improve HVAC efficiency and support the base's pursuit of low energy use. This system was the
first of its kind in the United States in 2022. The BTES system consists of 306 boreholes, each
drilled to a depth of 64 meters and spaced 6 meters apart on the surface (Hammock and Sullens,
2017). The boreholes are grouted and contain HDPE U-bend pipes to form a closed-loop borehole
heat exchanger. The boreholes are arranged in a 91-meter diameter circular pattern on the surface,
with hotter fluid stored in the outer zones of the circle and cooler fluid in the center (Figure 2-3).

This system heats and cools a three-story, 15,600m² administration building with a maximum
cooling load of 1,500kW (425 tons). As it is in a cooling-dominated region, for most of the year,
the building is cooled using water-to-water (geothermal) heat pumps connected to the BTES.
When heating is required, the heat pumps provide warmth to the building. During the cooler
season, chillers are used to capture and store "cooling energy" in the BTES. The BTES acts as a
thermal battery, storing excess cold from the winter underground for later use in the summer. This
system has reduced energy consumption by 47.5% compared to the existing air-source HVAC
system.

  Figure 2-3. Diagram of a UTES installation at a military base in Albany, Georgia (from
 Hammock and Sullens (2017)).

2.5 CASE STUDIES IN COLD REGIONS

The ground conditions common in cold regions, such as permafrost and extremely cold soils, can
significantly affect the potential for geothermal heating and cooling technologies. GSHP
technology is becoming increasingly effective and popular in cold climates, though these systems
often face an imbalance: they extract more heat from the ground during the winter than is returned
in the summer. In some cases, active thermal recharge of the ground during warmer months is
necessary to prevent depletion of thermal resources (Garber-Slaght and Keays, 2014). Seasonal
TES involves storing excess heat during the warmer summer months to use in the cold winter
months, which is particularly valuable in cold regions. While geothermal resources are utilized in
many Arctic nations, very few are located in the Arctic or subarctic regions of these countries
(Kolker et al., 2022). Figure 2-4 shows a map of geothermal resources in the Arctic region,
including areas where GSHP systems may be applicable. However, current technology and limited
research restrict the potential of GSHP systems in the far northern regions, where continuous, deep,
and very cold permafrost exists.
 8

Figure 2-4. Geothermal energy resources in the Arctic region (Kolker et al., 2022 used with
 permission).

2.5.1 United States of America - Alaska

The United States is considered an Arctic country due to the state of Alaska. Alaska spans four
climate zones, but the entire state is heating-dominated. The Arctic region of Alaska is underlain
by continuous permafrost, which can be quite deep (>600 meters) and remains very cold (below -
7°C in some areas) (Lachenbruch et al., 1982; Smith et al., 2010). To date, no geothermal heating
systems have been installed in the Alaskan Arctic.

GSHP systems have been installed in the non-Arctic regions of Alaska. These systems are
generally small and used primarily for heating. The subarctic region of Alaska, with its
discontinuous and near-thaw permafrost, has about 100 GSHP installations, though more are
expected in the future. In 2014, Garber-Slaght and Stevens conducted a survey of existing GSHP
systems in the Fairbanks North Star Borough (Garber-Slaght and Stevens, 2014). At that time,
approximately 40-50 GSHP systems were installed in the area. The survey focused on 13
installations across a variety of buildings and found that the building owners were satisfied with
their systems and reported savings on heating costs.

In 2014, Garber-Slaght and Keays experimented with a GSHP system that incorporated solar
thermal recharge for the ground heat exchanger (Garber-Slaght and Keays, 2014). A 17.6kW
water-to-air heat pump was installed at Weller Elementary School in Fairbanks, Alaska, designed
 9

to heat the school's ventilation air. Six 1.2 x 2.4m flat-plate solar thermal panels were installed
alongside the heat pump, using solar energy to recharge the horizontal 6-trench ground heat
exchanger during the summer months. Over the course of three years, the system achieved an
annual average coefficient of performance (CoP) of 3.7. The average entering fluid temperature to
the heat pump from the GHX was 9.2°C, which is significantly warmer than the typical entering
water temperature of 0°C in Fairbanks (Garber-Slaght, 2020; Garber-Slaght and Keays, 2014).
This system with thermal recharge served as a proof of concept for the potential of such systems
in the subarctic.

Further south in Alaska, a few larger-scale GSHP systems are in operation. One example is the
GSHP system at the Juneau Airport, which heats and cools the main terminal and even melts ice
on the sidewalks. This system includes 31 GSHP units and 108 wells, each 93m deep (Meyer et
al., 2011).

2.5.2     Canada

Canada has abundant opportunities for low-temperature GSHPs. In 2019, the installed capacity of
GSHPs in Canada totaled 1,822.5MWt and the total energy usage was 14,235 terajoules per year
(TJ/yr) (Thompson et al., 2020).

In 2006, one of the first BTES systems in North America was installed at Drake Landing, Alberta,
Canada for a community heating system of 52 single-family homes (Drakes Landing Solar
Community, 2022). The system uses 144 boreholes of 35m depth, the boreholes are arranged in
four quadrants each with a connection back to the energy center. Solar-heated water is used to
charge the BTES field when it is available. The energy center contains heat exchangers, solar
collectors, a back-up gas boiler, pumps, and controls. Each house receives heated water from the
energy center which goes through an air handler unit located in the basement that delivers heated
air to the houses. The full district system has a capacity of 4.5MW and has a demand of 50GJ per
house per year. The BTES reaches 80°C by the end of the summer (Canmet Energy, 2009).

2.5.3     Sweden

Sweden has a long history of utilizing geothermal heating and cooling systems. Typically, these
systems are small to medium-sized vertical closed-loop GSHP systems, primarily used for space
heating and domestic hot water in residential buildings (Lund and Toth, 2021). Some of these
systems are recharged using exhaust air or solar energy. Approximately a quarter of installed
systems in Sweden use horizontal ground loops, typically 1 meter deep. These systems freeze the
ground surrounding the coils, and the phase change that occurs during freezing provides additional
energy for the GSHP. Several district heating networks in Sweden also use large GSHP systems,
and the number of large GSHP and BTES systems increased by nearly 40% between 2015 and
2019. By 2019, Sweden had installed a total of 6,680MWt of GSHP capacity.

One example of a BTES installation in northern Sweden is a system in Luleå, which operated
between 1983 and 1989. This system featured 120 boreholes: each spaced 4 meters apart and

 10

extending to a depth of 65 meters. The system was used to store waste heat from a steel plant,
which was later used to heat a building during the winter (Nordell, 1994).

2.5.4     Finland

Heating with heat pumps is common in Finland, with an estimated 140,000 GSHP systems in
operation (Kallio, 2019). In 2020, the Finnish government set a goal to supply 10% of residential
heating with alternative energy sources. The total energy demand for space heating in Finland is
approximately 70-80 terawatt hours (TWh) annually, of which GSHP systems provide about 8 to
9%. One notable BTES system in Toholampi, Finland, uses waste heat from local industries and
solar heat to heat a community swimming pool. This system features 61 boreholes, each 45 meters
deep and spaced 2.5 meters apart (Arola et al., 2021). The project demonstrated that BTES systems
can be successful in cold regions like central Finland. The boreholes were drilled through 22 meters
of clay above hard gabbro and diorite bedrock. Boreholes were arranged in four rings, with BHEs
in each ring connected in parallel, and the rings themselves connected in series. Single U-pipe
collectors were installed into each borehole and filled with groundwater. The steel pipes were
anchored to the bedrock to minimize soil disturbance due to the thick soil layer. The system was
charged with heat continuously for 150 days, followed by 120 days of heat usage. The undisturbed
ground temperature was approximately 5°C, with permanent snow cover lasting between 130 to
160 days per year. Modeling simulations of the system, conducted using COMSOL software,
indicated that the system could operate with thermal storage efficiencies of up to 60%, based on
heat injection and discharge rates. Other BTES systems have reported typical efficiencies of 40-
50%, with few exceeding 60% efficiency (Arola et al., 2021).

2.5.5     Norway

Norway has numerous GSHP systems, including BTES installations for both heating and cooling.
In 2019, Midttømme et al. (2020) estimated that approximately 60,000 GSHP systems were
installed across the country. The total installed capacity was around 1,150MWt, with an annual
energy usage of approximately 12,600TJ/yr. Borehole depths typically range from 160 meters to
300 meters, with systems containing between 38 and 180 boreholes. More than 25 installations
feature a combined borehole length exceeding 10,000 meters.

2.6 GDHC AND BTES MODELING

Modeling large GSHP installations is crucial for designing optimal systems and ensuring efficient
operation. Given the high upfront cost of GSHP systems, effective design is essential to achieve
long-term energy cost savings. The design must consider the specific location and application, as
these factors can significantly influence system performance. Modeling subsurface and exit fluid
temperatures is vital for predicting the performance of different BTES configurations, which can
lead to more efficient GSHP system designs. Seasonal heating and cooling loads, for example, can
vary based on local climate, exposure to sunlight, building construction, intended use of climate-
controlled spaces, and other factors. Additionally, peak heating and cooling demands need to be
factored in alongside annual total energy demand. Due to significant variations, models must be

 11

carefully constructed using accurate data to ensure effective system design. Both analytical and
numerical models provide valuable insights when designing and evaluating BTES systems, as well
as their environmental impacts.

Modeling BHEs can be challenging due to their extreme slenderness (Diersch and Bauer, 2015).
The thermal efficiency of BHEs depends on several parameters, including the thermal conductivity
of the circulating fluid and the borehole, as well as borehole length and fluid flow rate (Ahmed et
al., 2022). As a result, models can be highly sensitive to these parameters. In cases where downhole
thermal conductivity is unknown or variable, simulating different conductivity scenarios can help
predict system performance.

Analytical models are often used to provide quick and reasonably accurate solutions by simplifying
the problem with appropriate assumptions. The concept of heat transfer via an infinite line source
(ILS) dates back to Lord Kelvin in 1882. The ILS model was later applied to BHE heat transfer
(Ingersoll and Plass, 1948; Ingersoll, Zobel, and Ingersoll, 1954). More recently, the finite line
source (FLS) model has been developed and proven suitable for predicting thermal response and
performance in multi-borehole systems (Claesson and Eskilson, 1988; Eskilson, 1986; Eskilson
and Claesson, 1988). The FLS model can be used to analyze the short-term behavior of borehole
exit fluid temperatures in BHEs (Javed and Claesson, 2011). In addition, FLS has been employed
to evaluate the long-term thermal response of BHE systems with varying borehole configurations
(Claesson and Javed, 2011).

GLHEPro is a widely-used design tool in the GSHP industry (Spitler, 2000), and it utilizes
Eskilson’s long time-step g-function method (Eskilson, 1987) for thermal response analysis.
However, Eskilson’s method and its variations do not account for the thermal capacitance of
borehole components such as the pipe and grout. Yavuzturk and Spitler (1999) developed a short
time-step g-function method, which was later refined by Xu and Spitler (2006) to incorporate a
one-dimensional numerical model that accounts for fluid thermal mass and convective resistance,
factors that vary with flow rate, fluid mixture, and fluid temperature. Despite this, assumptions
such as simplified groundwater presence and/or movement and uniform heat flux remain in place
when using GLHEPro.

Various modeling software are used to simulate GSHP, GDHC, and UTES systems, including
TOUGH, COMSOL, GLHEPro, and TRNSYS. Due to the complexity of GSHP systems, very
few, if any, model can comprehensively simulate heat transfer across the entire system, including
the ground, boreholes, heat exchanger loops, heat pump, and building. Some software focuses on
specific aspects of the system, like TOUGH on ground heat transfer, while others provide
simplified estimates for energy transfer across all components, such as GLHEPro.

For example, the Toholampi BTES system was modeled using COMSOL software, which
conducted simulations over 10 charging and 10 extraction phases, matching field data (Arola et
al., 2021). Similarly, a TRNSYS model was used to simulate a system in Kuujjuaq, Canada, which
included 100 boreholes and a 1,000m² solar array for heating a drinking water pumping station
(Giordano and Raymond, 2019). Catolico et al. (2016) used the TOUGH software for sensitivity
 12

analysis of soil thermal and hydrological parameters in BTES systems combined with solar
thermal. Another interesting modeling approach is the coupling of Modelica Buildings Library
with TOUGH, as presented by Hu et al. (2020). This coupled model supports the design and sizing
of district heating and cooling systems with geothermal energy storage in borehole fields. The
Modelica-TOUGH coupled model was validated with a g-function-based Modelica model,
demonstrating its ability to represent the entire closed-loop system, from ground thermal response
to changes in building load.

Recent modeling efforts have focused on comparing the performance of BTES systems with real-
world data. FEFLOW, a finite element modeling software, was used to simulate the performance
of an experimental BTES installation in Paskov, Czech Republic (Rapantova et al., 2016). The
BTES consists of a four-by-four grid of 60m deep boreholes spaced between 2.5m and 5m apart
with double U-pipes in each. The study found a good correlation between the simulated
temperatures and in-situ measurements, highlighting the reliability of the model in predicting
temperature trends. Similarly, the performance of the well-known Drake’s Landing Solar
Community BTES in Okotoks, Alberta, Canada, was modeled using the TOUGH2 software
(Catolico et al., 2016). Using the model, a sensitivity analysis was conducted to determine the
impact of small changes to soil thermal conductivity, background groundwater flow, soil
permeability, and soil saturation on BTES heat extraction efficiency. The sensitivity analysis
conducted as part of this study showed that BTES efficiency increases with lower soil thermal
conductivity and saturation levels, as well as higher soil permeability and groundwater flow.

The impact of groundwater flow on BTES systems has been another focus of recent modeling
studies. For example, a study by Chiasson et al. (2000) used a finite element model to simulate the
effects of groundwater flow on a BTES system. The study found that even moderate groundwater
flow rates can have significant impacts on seasonal subsurface temperatures. Another study used
COMSOL Multiphysics for long-term (50-year) and short-term (1-hour) simulations of a large
BHE (Zanchini et al., 2012). The long-term results showed that the system eventually reached a
steady state, despite unbalanced heating and cooling loads, and heat diffusion became more
dominant than advection due to groundwater flow.

2.7 MODELING APPROACH FOR THE DEMONSTRATION OF A BTES SYSTEM
 AT FORT WAINWRIGHT, AK

The project's modeling strategy was a comprehensive, multi-stage process designed to move from
initial load calculation to a full, long-term environmental impact assessment. This workflow
systematically integrated four distinct modeling tools: it began with EnergyPlus to define the
building energy loads, followed by GLHEPro for preliminary borehole field sizing. It then utilized
a more flexible Superposition Borehole Method for detailed system design under variable heat-
injection conditions. Finally, it employed GeoStudio to conduct a long-term (25-year) simulation
of the subsurface thermal plume and its interaction with groundwater, a task for which the more
computationally intensive COMSOL Multiphysics was considered but ultimately replaced due to
project scale.

 13

 Table 1. Project modeling approach.

 Stage Model Purpose and key activities
 Building EnergyPlus The process collected building specifications and energy use data for input into
 Load an EnergyPlus model, which to calculated the realistic and validated heating
 Calculation and cooling demands of the target buildings. These building loads served as
 the primary input data for the initial system sizing.
 Initial Sizing GLHEPro Using the loads from EnergyPlus, GLHEPro was employed to generate a
 preliminary design. It estimated the required number and depth of boreholes
 needed to meet the buildings' energy demands over the system's lifespan.
 Detailed Superposition Since GLHEPro's structure was too rigid for designing a system with variable
 Design Borehole heat injection, a more flexible hybrid numerical and analytical model was
 Method used. This "Superposition Borehole Method" allowed for a more detailed and
 adaptable system design.
 Long-term GeoStudio The final step was to simulate the long-term (25-year) impact of the BTES on
 simulation the subsurface. While a COMSOL Multiphysics model was initially
 considered, it was deemed too computationally demanding for the large scale
 of the project. Therefore, GeoStudio was selected to model the thermal plume,
 groundwater flow, and thermodynamics, including freeze/thaw cycles.

3.0 PERFORMANCE OBJECTIVES
Fort Wainwright uses 80% of its building energy for space heating (Figure 3-1). Located in
Fairbanks, Alaska, Fort Wainwright relies on an aging coal-fired combined heat and power plant
(CHP), with 89% of its total energy consumption sourced from coal (Figure 3-1). Annually,
roughly 25% of the CHP output is for electricity, while approximately 52% is for steam heat (the
other 23% is lost to inefficiencies of the aging system). Dependence on a single energy source for
all installation functions undermines resilience. To enhance resilience and reduce energy demand
on the post, alternative heating energy sources are necessary.

 b)

Figure 3-1. Fort Wainwright a) electricity and space heating energy sources, and b) energy
 source types.

While retrofitting the district heating system from coal produced steam to a GSHP will increase
the electrical load, the impact is manageable. A CoP of 3 is considered average for basic GSHP
systems in Alaska (Meyer et al., 2011) and incorporating UTES and thermal recharge from the
CHP excess heat would lessen the impact. Furthermore, lowering the installation’s overall space
 15

heating demand will reduce the thermal load on the existing CHP, which is operating near capacity.
This directly supports the future design of a smaller, more efficient CHP system. Additionally, a
proven GSHP system in the Fairbanks climate will encourage the adoption of this technology at
installations in extreme cold climates. Table 3-1 summarizes our performance objectives.

4.0 FACILITY / SITE DESCRIPTION
4.1 SITE LOCATION

Fort Wainwright is located in Fairbanks, Alaska, in the Interior region of the state at 64.80°N
(Figure 4-1); Fairbanks experiences a continental sub-Arctic climate, with a mean annual air
temperature of −2.1°C (monthly climate normal from 1991–2020) and a monthly average
temperature range of −27.8°C to 23.3°C (public data from National Oceanic and Atmospheric
Administration, NOAA). Limited areas of Fort Wainwright are underlain by discontinuous
permafrost that is on the verge of thawing.

The installation currently uses a steam-to-glycol district heating system (Figure 4-1), with most
buildings receiving hydronic heat at temperatures between 71 to 82°C. Buildings 3001 and 3002
are newer buildings, have in floor radiant heat in portions of the buildings and are collocated with
potential areas for a BTES (Figure 4-1).

 Figure 4-1. Fairbanks (west), and Fort Wainwright (east). The location of selected
buildings used in this study. Location of Fairbanks is shown in the inset of the upper right
corner. (Powered by Esri, HERE, Garmin, Intermap, increment P Corp., GEBCO, USGS,
 FAO, NPS, NRCAN, GeoBase, IGN, Kadaster NL, Ordnance Survey, Esri China (Hong
 Kong), (c) OpenStreetMap contributors, and the GIS User Community.)
 17

4.2 SITE GEOLOGY/HYDROGEOLOGY

Fairbanks lies within the Tanana Valley watershed geohydrologic unit, which is entirely located
within a discontinuous permafrost zone. The majority of surficial materials in the Fairbanks
mesoscale area consist of various unconsolidated sediments, ranging from silts to gravels. The
region is seismically active, though no major faults or significant deep circulation have been
identified. Subsurface transport behavior is complex and difficult to map due to the heterogeneity
in hydrogeologic features, which can vary from one location to another. This variability is
influenced by factors such as the presence or absence of permafrost, surface-subsurface
interaction, and varying depth to bedrock.

A high-resolution permafrost map is not available, and the exact distribution of permafrost at any
given location remains uncertain. Permafrost has been encountered as deep as 80 meters in
Fairbanks, though it is possible that deeper formations exist (Anderson, 1970). Site-specific
investigations are necessary to avoid cryohydrologic formations.

The geohydrologic unit primarily consists of unconsolidated alluvial aquifers and heterogeneous
hydrofacies, with significant connectivity between groundwater and surface water, especially near
the surface (Callegary et al., 2013). Direct evidence of this interaction in Fairbanks includes
reversal of flow directions, depending on forcing conditions (Hinzman et al., 2000). These
interactions are expected to be more tightly coupled near larger hydrological features, such as the
Chena River (Figure 4-1), which is of particular interest here due to its proximity to the chosen
site and its seasonal variability.

Depth to bedrock tends to be deeper further south, while surficial exposures of basement rock and
schists are found north of the town, as the geography transitions from the floodplain valley to the
edge of the geohydrologic unit. It is not anticipated that bedrock will be encountered at the chosen
site.

The Tanana River to the south and elevated terrain to the north form the mesoscale geohydrologic
boundaries, but dominant groundwater pathways at any specific site are governed by local features.
In Fairbanks, the seasonal variation of the Chena River, along with intermittently distributed
cryohydrologic features, results in complex transport behavior. Figure 4-2 illustrates the
generalized groundwater conditions from south to north in Fairbanks (Anderson, 1970).

Previously measured data for the site and surrounding mesoscale area is limited. The available
information is useful for identifying trends and making first-order estimates of potential subsurface
conditions at the site. Studies aimed at identifying transport paths in the area have had limited
success due to the complexity of the subsurface. Most logged wells are shallow water wells that
do not intersect bedrock, providing only limited information on overburden material and no data
on ground thermal properties.

 18

Figure 4-2. Cross-section of groundwater conditions at Fairbanks (image reproduced from
 Anderson (1970). Public domain).

Lawson et al. (1996) mapped a complex, discontinuous permafrost area near Canol Road at Fort
Wainwright, approximately 1,000 meters north of our study site (Figure 4-3). This area exhibits
extremely complex transport behavior, with "aquifers occurring above, below, and adjacent to
permanently frozen materials, as well as within thaw zones bounded by permafrost" (Lawson et
al., 1996). While the Canol Road study may serve as a useful proxy, its applicability as a direct
analog is limited due to its location on the opposite side of the Chena River, where discontinuous
permafrost exists, and bedrock is much closer to the surface (Figure 4-3).

Figure 4-3. Study area from Lawson et al. 1996 relative to the demonstration site (left) and
 a detail view of the previous study (right, image reproduced from Lawson et al. 1996.
 Public Domain).

 19

In a study examining water wells in the area, Lilly and Rayl (1987) found evidence suggesting
generally slow water flow in the subsurface, despite its complexity. This implies that groundwater
convection across wellbores may be limited. Their interpretation of the generalized north-south
hydrology aligns with Anderson’s 1970 findings, as shown in Figure 4-2.

For this study, a database of U.S. Army Corps of Engineers (USACE) bore logs were collected
and transcribed. This curated collection of subsurface data for Fairbanks is unique to this study, as
the bore logs have not been previously published or digitized. The recorded data generally
corroborates mesoscale trends but are not reliable enough to create high-resolution stratigraphic or
hydrologic maps of the subsurface in Fairbanks.

The database consists of approximately 1,300 drill logs, down sampled from about 2,000 available
logs. Most of these drill logs correspond to borings drilled to a depth of 20 meters or less, with the
deepest reaching about 90 meters. Of the interpreted drill logs, only those north of the Chena River
intersect bedrock (83 logs in total). Additionally, only 250 drill logs record the initial depth of the
water table. While this represents the most robust set of direct measurements available, the data
should be considered coarse. Wells were drilled by multiple companies over several years, and
during different times of the year, which could have introduced errors in recording, inconsistencies
in soil classification, and other unanticipated inaccuracies. Figure 4-4 shows the locations of each
boring in the database, including the selected site, and Figure 4-5 shows the distribution of well
depths reported in the available logs.

The logs generally report overburden consisting of silts, sands, and gravels, classified using the
Unified Soil Classification System (USCS). However, it is unclear whether bedrock was
consistently reported for every log, as there is some inconsistency in the reported depth to bedrock.
Wells near the selected site offer useful data for considering surrogate strata layers in modeling,
but this interpretation should be regarded as coarse. Some drill logs mention encountering frozen
ground, but these reports are sparse and do not provide enough data to extrapolate meaningfully
for the study area.

Based on the reported initial potentiometric surface levels in the wells, the study area appears to
lie in a stable zone. Within the valley, the water table seems to be in relative equilibrium, with no
significant hydraulic head gradient present. However, as one moves northward out of the valley, a
more pronounced gradient may emerge, likely due to changes in elevation. The well logs, however,
may be too dispersed by season and year of drilling to serve as reliable quantitative indicators of
potentiometric surface, but the stable zone south of the river and the more significant gradient to
the north align with the findings of Lawson et al. (1996) in their study area.

The database does not account for seasonal variations or interactions with the Chena River. Initial
field measurements, which monitored both the river and nearby wells, show some evidence of
coupling between the water levels near the site and the river in the shallow subsurface. The extent
and depth of this coupling, however, remain uncertain.

 20

 Figure 4-4. Reported locations of wells in the curated database.

 Figure 4-5. Reported depths of wells in the project database.
 21

In late summer 2023, groundwater levels were monitored continuously (every 15 minutes) for
about six weeks across Fort Wainwright. Fourteen Solinst groundwater level loggers were
deployed (Figure 4-6) and the data were barometrically corrected (Figure 4-7). Wells in the eastern
part of the site exhibited higher water levels than those in the west, reflecting the hydraulic gradient
and the flow direction of the Chena River from east to west. Data from this monitoring effort show
a roughly 0.5-meter difference in water level over a 1-kilometer span in the east-west direction.
However, this gradient is complicated by variations in the north-south direction.

It is anticipated that the bedrock at the study site lies deeper than it does north of the Chena River.
A simple estimate, based on known bedrock depths, suggests that the bedrock at the study site is
at least 76 meters deep (Figure 4-8). This estimate for the depth to bedrock is conservative and so
the bedrock may be deeper. According to the drill logs, none of the wells south of the Chena River
intersect bedrock. The actual transition in bedrock depth from north to south could be highly
nonlinear or may not correspond directly to the schists encountered to the north.

Figure 4-6. Groundwater wells with water level monitoring installed. The test buildings are
 circled.

 22

 Figure 4-7. Groundwater levels at wells monitored during this study.

 23

 Figure 4-8. Estimated bedrock depth (red cross) from well measurements (blue solid dot).

4.3 GROUND THERMAL PROPERTIES

Hydrogeological conditions play a crucial role in determining the rate of heat transfer between
boreholes and the surrounding formation, the amount of heat that can be stored within a formation,
and the accuracy of the measured thermal properties. Under conditions with advective fluid
transport, effects of groundwater can dominate over heat conduction through the soil or rock
material. The analysis in this work is limited to closed-loop GSHPs and BTES; open-loop storage
systems are outside the scope of this work.

For system design, a baseline understanding of the ground’s thermal properties is essential. Ideally,
these properties would be determined more accurately through well testing at the site, but typical
values for different geological materials are well-documented and can provide a useful starting
point. Table 4-1 shows the thermal properties for various soils, as summarized by Chiasson et al.
(2000), with original data from Hellstrom (1991). Values for rocks (e.g., sandstone, shale) are
omitted, as evidence suggests the chosen site consists predominantly of unconsolidated sediments.

Groundwater flow introduces advective transport processes, where the movement of water past
wellbores results in faster heat transfer between the formation and borehole compared to
conduction alone. Advection increases with higher groundwater flow rates, which are linked to
hydraulic conductivity and hydraulic head. Previous research has shown that advection from
groundwater flow can significantly affect closed-loop borehole systems placed in materials with
high hydraulic conductivity, such as sandstones, gravels, and rocks (Chiasson et al., 2000). Table
4-1, also presents typical hydraulic conductivity values for various soils, summarized by Chiasson
et al. (2000) and attributed to Domenico and Schwartz (1997). In terms of thermal conductivity,
saturated values have been found to range between 2-4W/m·°C in the relevant literature
(Buntebarth, 2020; Darrow, 2010; Kersten, 1948).

 24

 Table 4-1. Thermal and hydrological properties of soils (Chiasson et al., 2000; Domenico
 and Schwartz, 1997).

 Thermal Volumetric Heat Hydraulic
 Soil Conductivity Capacity Conductivity
                            (W/m-K)                    (J/m3-K)                           (m/s)
     Gravel                  0.70 – 0.90                  1.40 x 106              3.0 x 10-4 – 3.0 x 10-2
     Coarse Sand             0.70 – 0.90                  1.40 x 106              9.0 x 10-7 – 6.0 x 10-3
     Fine Sand               0.70 – 0.90                  1.40 x 106              2.0 x 10-7 – 2.0 x 10-4
     Silt                    1.20 – 2.40                  2.85 x 106              1.0 x 10-9 – 2.0 x 10-5
     Clay                    0.85 – 1.10                  3.30 x 106              1.0 x 10-11 – 4.7 x 10-9

Advection of surrounding groundwater is a concern in the context of BTES systems because it can
reduce the efficiency of the system by moving useful heat away into unrecoverable areas.
Groundwater advection lowers performance, and over 50% of the heat is typically lost seasonally
(Skarphagen et al., 2019). In regions with discontinuous permafrost, advection is also a concern
when storing heat due to the potential for heat from the borehole field to travel significantly
downstream, further dissipating energy. Table 4-2 shows the typical travel distances of heat in
different soil types under a 0.01-meter hydraulic head gradient (Skarphagen et al., 2019).

 Table 4-2. Travel distance of heat under 0.01-meter head gradient (Skarphagen et al.,
 2019).

 Hydraulic Heat Travel Heat Travel Distance,
 Conductivity Distance, 6 months 12 months
                        (m/s)                     (m)                          (m)
                       1.16 x 10-7                  0                           0.1
 -6
                       1.16 x 10                   0.3                          0.6
                       1.16 x 10-5                 3.2                          6.4
                       1.16 x 10-4                 31.9                        63.7
 -3
                       1.16 x 10                   319                         637

                       1.16 x 10-2                3,186                        6,372

An effective way to capture the potential importance of advective forces is by considering the
Peclet number of the borehole field and the surrounding geologic medium. The Peclet number is
a dimensionless parameter that represents the ratio of advective to diffusive transport for a given
process. In the case of sands and gravels, typical Peclet numbers indicate advective dominance
(Chiasson et al., 2000). Rocks, on the other hand, generally have low Peclet numbers, suggesting
that bedrock is a more favorable medium for installing seasonal thermal storage systems—if
available—regardless of cost. Thermal conductivity estimates tend to be artificially high when

 25

measured under high Peclet conditions (Chiasson et al., 2000). A detailed method for calculating
Peclet numbers in closed-loop GSHP systems is presented by Chiasson et al. (2000).

Advection enhances heat transfer between the formation and the borehole, and this transfer can
occur in both directions. In conventional heat pump systems, where heat is extracted directly from
the ground, advection can provide desired ground temperatures and increase the amount of energy
extracted. However, when storing heat in cold regions, such as at Fort Wainwright, advection could
accelerate the thermal degradation of the ground.

Modeling groundwater flow in cold regions is complex due to nonlinear freeze-thaw effects and
intricate thermal boundary conditions. Thawing, in particular, can instantly increase hydraulic
conductivity, which, in turn, boosts advective forces (Lamontagne‐Hallé et al., 2020). There are
also challenges in real-time monitoring of hydrological processes in cold regions (Ireson et al.,
2012), which complicates the diagnosis of any observed behavior. Additionally, changes in water
usage could alter the hydraulic head gradient over time.

The subsurface at Fort Wainwright is complex, and no major studies on thermal gradients have
been conducted in the area. In 2009, a thermal response test (TRT) following the American Society
of Heating, Refrigeration, and Air-Conditioning Engineers (ASHRAE) guidelines was performed
at Fort Wainwright, near the study site. This test provided an estimate of the thermal properties of
the unconsolidated sediment formations. The 48-hour test was conducted in a borehole with a
diameter of 0.14 meters and a total length of approximately 78 meters. The initial undisturbed
ground formation temperature ranged from 2.5 to 2.9°C. The collected data were analyzed using
the line source method, and estimated properties and formation logs are listed in Table 4-3 and
Table 4-4 (GRTI, 2009).

 Table 4-3. Thermal properties as determined from a TRT at Fort Wainwright (GRTI,
                                       2023, 2009).

                        (J/m3-K)      (W/m-K)            (m2/day)

                2009    2.21 x 106        2.68              0.105              2.5 - 2.9
                2023    2.14 x 106        2.82              0.113              4.2 - 5.3

To gather additional subsurface data near the study site, another TRT was conducted in 2023
through this project (GRTI, 2023). This test was performed in a bore field previously used for a
GSHP system in a residential building at Fort Wainwright (Figure 4-9). Worth noting, limited
information was available about the pipe distribution underground horizontally between the house
and the boreholes, which may have led to introduced errors into the final values of the soil
properties from this test. The 72-hour test followed ASHRAE guidelines, and subsurface details

from this test are listed in Table 4-3 and Table 4-4. The heat capacity, thermal conductivity, and
thermal diffusivity results from the 2023 test were similar to those from the 2009 test, though there

Comparing with Table 4-1, values measured at Fort Wainwright through TRT resulted in slightly
higher values for heat capacity and thermal conductivity in sands and gravels. These discrepancies
could stem from several factors, including user error, groundwater advection, and other variables.
 27

The values presented here provide a baseline for unfrozen, unconsolidated sediments in the area.
However, to achieve a more accurate determination of local transport properties, further site-
specific thermal response testing and hydraulic potential mapping are recommended.

4.4     BUILDINGS 3001 AND 3002

The existing hydronic heat delivery temperatures at Fort Wainwright exceed the efficient operating
range of most heat pumps. Buildings with the potential for lower temperature delivery systems are
more suitable for heat pump retrofits and were the focus for this project. Buildings 3001, a
company operations facility, and 3002, a Battalion Headquarters (Figure 4-10), are relatively new
and centrally located. Both buildings feature in-floor, low-temperature hydronic heating systems.
Built in 2016, both buildings have two floors, with total areas of 1,393m² for Building 3001 and
1,895m² for Building 3002. These buildings are situated on the southwest side of the runway, near
a large expanse of green space that could serve as a potential location for a borehole field (Figure
4-6).

                                     Figure 4-10. Building 3002.

Construction documents for Buildings 3001 and 3002 were analyzed to inform the development
of building envelope components, including opaque ceiling and wall assemblies, as well as HVAC
system operations for the energy model using EnergyPlus.

Thermal zones were established to determine the buildings' thermal loads and annual energy
consumption. Typically, thermal zones are divided into interior core and perimeter zones based on
cardinal directions. However, due to the complexity of Buildings 3001 and 3002, especially the
variety of HVAC systems and distribution networks, as well as the quantity and locations of
thermostats—more thermal zones were required than in a simplified approach.

The spatial configuration of these thermal zones, based on HVAC system diversity, allows for the
evaluation of system integration in isolated areas without affecting the accuracy of other building
sections. For example, if the GSHP’s output is only used to provide heat through radiant flooring
or baseboard systems, zones without those heating distribution systems can be excluded, ensuring
 28

a more precise analysis of peak load. Figure 4-11 through Figure 4-12 show the thermal zones for
both buildings.

b)
