5.0 TEST DESIGN
Developing a conceptual GDHC design requires expertise across several disciplines, from geology
to engineering. The information for each topic is detailed in the following sections and will be
integrated to form a cohesive design and model of the system.

5.1 PRE-STUDY FIELD VALIDATION

This project did not intend to install a full-scale GDHC system; rather, it was a desktop study
aimed at assessing the feasibility of implementing such a system at Fort Wainwright, Alaska.
Given the challenges posed by the cold ground temperatures and moving groundwater in the
region, there were concerns regarding the potential performance of a heat pump system in these
less than ideal conditions. To explore and address some of these concerns, an existing heat pump
system in Fairbanks (operational since 2012) was evaluated. This system is located in the same
floodplain as the proposed Fort Wainwright site, making it a relevant case study for comparison.

The existing system is a 35kW (10-ton) GSHP designed to heat a 465m² commercial garage, a
connected office building, and a small apartment. The ground heat exchanger for this system
consists of 10 boreholes, each reaching a depth of 45m. The boreholes penetrate through frozen
gravel and extend into a layer of moving groundwater, which provides a dynamic environment for
heat transfer. A system overview of the Fairbanks installation is shown in Figure 5-1.

 Figure 5-1. Diagram of the GSHP system at a 465m 2 commercial building in south
 Fairbanks, Alaska (from Garber-Slaght and Stevens, 2014 with permission).

By studying the performance of this system, insights into the practical challenges and effectiveness
of GSHP systems in similar conditions informed our assessment of the viability of such a system

 31

for Fort Wainwright. The system's performance in Fairbanks serves as an important point of
reference for understanding how a heat pump system could potentially operate in the unique
subsurface conditions of Fort Wainwright, especially in terms of heat transfer efficiency,
groundwater movement, and seasonal variation in temperature.

5.1.1 Sampling Methods

A data collection system was retrofitted into the existing heat pump system to evaluate the
longevity and performance of a heating-only heat pump under a worst-case permafrost scenario.
The purpose of this system was to monitor key parameters such as electrical use and the fluid
temperatures entering and exiting the heat pump. To ensure the reliability of the system, it was
important to verify that the fluid flow remained consistent throughout the operation of the heat
pump. To achieve this, several fluid flow measurements were recorded over the course of an 18-
month data collection period. Table 5-1 provides detailed information for each data point and the
corresponding sensor used to gather the data, ensuring that all critical aspects of the system's
operation were monitored. This included temperature readings from various points along the
system's loop, as well as flow rates to ensure that the constant flow conditions required for accurate
analysis were maintained.

5.1.2     Results

The layout shown in Figure 5-2 was used to record data, with sensors taking measurements every
two minutes. The heat pump's power consumption, measured in watts, was used to calculate the
total energy in kilowatt-hours (kWh). For the three loops—tank, ground loop, and building—the
temperatures of the return and supply lines were recorded. These temperature measurements were
then used to calculate the kWh values with the following equations:

temperature fluctuations, averaging around 2.3 to 2.5 during the summer and decreasing to
approximately 2.0 in the winter. This trend is expected due to the influence of outdoor temperatures
on GSHP performance. It is also worth noting that the temperature sensors had a margin of error,
which is reflected in the CoP calculations, as shown in Figure 5-4.

Electricity costs were calculated based on the kWh consumed by the GSHP, using the current local

local building, while the average price for Alaska was sourced from the U.S. Energy Information
Administration’s online database of historical prices (U.S. Energy Information Administration,
2024). The natural gas cost calculations assumed a furnace efficiency of 96%.

It is important to note that the Fairbanks natural gas cost data included additional fees, as there
was no way to isolate the base cost of natural gas. Despite this limitation, the table shows that the
GSHP’s electricity costs were lower than the natural gas costs based on Fairbanks pricing.
However, when compared to the average statewide natural gas prices, heating with natural gas was
less expensive.

5.2 CONCEPTUAL EXPERIMENT DESIGN

The conceptual design began with a detailed building energy model using EnergyPlus, an open-
source software provided by the U.S. Department of Energy’s (DOE) Building Technologies
Office (BTO). This energy model supplied load information and estimated potential energy
savings from switching to a heat pump system. An analytical model of the BTES system was then
created, first using GLHEPro, then directly with the superposition borehole method, based on the
building loads from EnergyPlus and potential heat recharge options. Analytical modeling
determined the size and configuration of the BTES required. A 3D numerical simulation using
COMSOL was employed to model a single BHE, providing insights into the temperature profiles
of each borehole (Oh et al., 2025). Lastly, a subsurface numerical model of the area was developed
using Temp/W, a sub-model within the commercially available GeoStudio Flow 3D suite. The
GeoStudio model helped refine the analytical models by offering a more detailed understanding
of the subsurface thermal changes associated with the BTES system.

5.2.1 Baseline Characterization of Thermal Loads – EnergyPlus building models

Building heating and cooling loads, along with associated energy consumption, were assessed
using the EnergyPlus building energy simulation program, employing typical meteorological year
(TMY) weather data for Fairbanks, AK. Architectural geometry and thermal zone inputs were
incorporated into the EnergyPlus input data file (IDF) using the Euclid extension for SketchUp.
Additional configuration of material properties, internal gains, architectural and HVAC objects,
and controls were manually written within the IDF file. Figure 5-6 shows a 3D rendering of both
buildings.

The winter heating energy consumption results from the EnergyPlus simulation were compared
against actual steam energy usage data provided by the Fort Wainwright Directorate of Public
Works (DPW) for each building. This comparison was conducted to verify model assumptions and
validate the building load outputs for the GSHP simulation.

Since TMY weather data is not suitable for accurately comparing simulated and actual energy
usage, a custom EnergyPlus Weather (EPW) file was created using actual weather data from the
same date range as the provided steam data to calibrate the model.

For Building 3001, quasi-weekly steam usage data, manually collected from January to March
2023, was used for validation. For Building 3002, 15-minute steam usage data collected by the
building’s control system from February to May 2023 was available. EnergyPlus results were
output at the same timestep as the collected data for a detailed comparison. Some anomalies in the
data were observed, especially as exterior temperatures warmed, likely due to issues with the
condensate flow meter. Outlier data showing excessive usage was statistically filtered out for
February and March. However, data from April through July was considered impractical for
comparison due to the unresolved flow meter issues.

 37

 a)

 b)

 Figure 5-6. EnergyPlus thermal model construction of Building a) 3001, and b) 3002.

Calibration strategies for outside ventilation air, envelope infiltration, and uncontrolled air
exchange from occupant activities were refined using Building 3002, as the available data for this
building was more detailed than for Building 3001. Graphical comparisons of the actual and
simulated energy use over two weeks with unique weather data are shown in Figure 5-7.

Root mean square error (RMSE) and mean bias error (MBE) were used to assess the accuracy of
the comparison between actual and simulated energy values. Following ASHRAE Guideline 14
(American Society of Heating, 2014), the monthly RMSE and MBE values were calibrated to
within 15% and 5%, respectively (Table 5-4).

After calibration adjustments were made to the building models, simulated outcomes were
generated for a full year using TMY data. Internal gains from occupants, plug loads, and electrical
appliances were estimated, with particular attention to computer and server rooms, which
contribute significant internal gains and thus drive cooling loads during the winter months. As the
size of the future GSHP was not yet determined, heating and cooling usage, along with peak loads,
 39

were output as thermal energy for each thermal zone. This approach, combined with the detailed
thermal zoning strategy, will facilitate the matching of the heat pump’s capacity to the appropriate
zones and enable simplified model reruns once a unit is selected. Whole-building simulation
results for Buildings 3001 and 3002 are shown in Figure 5-8.

Figure 5-8. Simulated heating and cooling energy loads and peak heating and cooling loads
 for the a) Company Operations Facility (3001), and the b) Battalion Headquarters
                                    buildings (3002).

The analysis projects substantial energy savings from the proposed geothermal heat pump
installation. Figure 5-9 shows the annual heating energy before and after this conversion. The 'pre'
value represents the current heat load, while 'post' is the projected load after conversion. The
models indicate a significant energy reduction: 36% annual heating energy reduction for Building
3002 and a 55% reduction for Building 3001. These impressive savings are a direct result of the
system's high efficiency, achieving a CoP of 3. It is important to note that this analysis focuses
specifically on the building zones compatible with low-temperature heat delivery.

 40

 Figure 5-9. Annual building and zone heating for current load (“Pre” installation) and
 after the heat pump installation (“Post” installation) for Buildings 3001 and 3002.

5.2.2 GSHP Modeling of Fort Wainwright, AK

Models offer a preliminary assessment of long-term system performance and subsurface
temperature trends including the expected impact of heat injection on the subsurface. Factors such
as groundwater flow, phase change of water freezing within the subsurface, and the impact of this
phase change on groundwater flow are critical considerations when modeling a BTES system for
Fairbanks. While groundwater flow such as from the nearby Chena River may help a typical GSHP
system recover from seasonal imbalance, this is not the case for storage-focused systems.

The modeling process began with an initial system design based on building loads derived from
the EnergyPlus model (Section 5.2.1), the physical space requirements for the borehole field, and
the system’s ability to meet heating and cooling demand over an expected 25-year lifespan. The
modeling effort considered two potential locations on Fort Wainwright. The first location is a
rectangular plot measuring 220 meters by 40 meters, located directly across the street from
Buildings 3001 and 3002 to the south (see Figure 5-10). The main advantage of this option is its
proximity to the buildings, though the limited space restricts optimal wellbore spacing. The second
location is approximately 1,100 meters from the buildings and features a larger area, approximately
250 meters by 200 meters. While the larger plot offers more flexibility, it may incur higher costs
due to the increased piping distance. The district heating system piping that supplies heat to the
buildings is located close to this area (Figure 4-1).

 41

 Figure 5-10. Potential installation locations for modeling study.

The conceptualized system is intended to be brought online at the end of the heating season, around
the first of May, and will charge the subsurface with heat over the summer months. This process
uses excess steam generated by the power plant, which is routed to the GHX via a steam-to-glycol
heat exchanger installed in the utilidor steam delivery system. This exchanger will route excess
heat to the GHX during the warmer months when the power plant is operating but building heat is
not needed. However, as the coal-fired power plant nears the end of its operational life, its eventual
replacement could reduce the amount of excess heat available. In light of this possibility, one
scenario investigated charging the BTES system for only the first five years of its operation. While
other small and diverse sources of excess heat exist (such as from cooling a nearby ice rink) they
were excluded from the models for simplification.

The modeling effort presented in this report utilized three distinct models. Initially, GLHEPro was
used to estimate a number of boreholes requirements including number, depth, and key parameters
to meet the heating loads calculated in the EnergyPlus model. While GLHEPro proved effective
for initial borehole sizing, its rigid structure limited its application to the design of a BTES system
involving adding heat directly to the ground loop over different intervals. Therefore, a second
model utilizing the superposition borehole method was adopted; this hybrid numerical and
analytical approach forms the basis for GLHEPro itself. Finally, a finite element model using
GeoStudio was used to study the extent and dynamic behavior of the subsurface thermal plume
over a 25-year period, accounting for groundwater flow, freezing/thawing processed, and the
associated latent heat effects on groundwater temperature.

5.2.2.1 GLHEPro Modeling
GLHEPro was selected as an off-the-shelf software solution for the design and long-term
performance modeling of potential BHE arrangements. When provided with heating and cooling
loads, along with subsurface heat transfer parameters, GLHEPro calculates the required borehole
depth and estimates the ground loop fluid temperatures after years of operation in a GSHP system.
For this project, GLHEPro was used to determine the number of boreholes and the size of the heat
pump required to meet the energy needs of Buildings 3001 and 3002. Monthly heating and cooling
loads, derived from the whole building EnergyPlus models (Section 5.2.1) were incorporated into
the GLHEPro model to help determine the appropriate system sizing. Peak load durations for
 42

heating and cooling were estimated to be 24 hours and 12 hours, respectively, based on the Peak
Load Analysis in GLHEPro.

To simulate the system's efficiency, a commercial heat pump was selected from GLHEPro’s
library. The software supplied 32-ton Econar GW3800 model, a hydronic heat pump supporting
entering water temperatures ranging from approximately -4°C to 38°C and flow of 54-gallon-per-
minute, was chosen for this study. An assumption of 2.5 gpm/ton was used to calculate the flow
required to meet the building heating and cooling loads. Borehole thermal resistance was
calculated using a borehole diameter of 14cm and a standard 3.8cm U-tube configuration. The
ground loop fluid in the system was assumed to be a 20% methanol and 80% water mixture (this
assumption was based on the currently installed systems in the Fairbanks area which use methanol;
follow on simulations use a glycol mixture). For ground parameters, the thermal conductivity was
set to 2.68W/m·K, based on the 2009 Thermal Response Test (TRT) at Fort Wainwright (Section
4.3), and the volumetric heat capacity was set to 2,213kJ/m³·°C. The undisturbed ground
temperature was assumed to be 2.4°C.

To address the severely imbalanced heating and cooling loads, the model was run with
supplemental heating sources of 30kW, 60kW, and 90kW. The borehole configuration for the
30kW and 60kW supplemental loads was a 2 by 12 rectangular grid, while the 90kW supplemental
load was modeled using a 2 by 6 rectangular grid (Table 5-5). The distance between borehole
centers was set to 19.8 meters in all cases. All cases fit within Location 1. Borehole depths
calculated by GLHEPro were 107.3 meters, 54.4 meters, and 51.7 meters for the 30kW, 60kW,
and 90kW scenarios, respectively.

 Table 5-5. GLHEPro model configurations.

 Supplemental Borehole
 Borehole Total Length
 Heat Added Depth
 Configuration (m)
 (kW) (m)
                       30                 2 by 12             107.3             2,575
                       60                 2 by 12             54.4              1,306
                       90                  2 by 6             51.7               620

The GLHEPro modeling results indicated that, without supplemental heating, the minimum peak
fluid temperature exiting the borehole system would drop to approximately -4°C within 10 years.
In the 30kW and 60kW cases, the maximum peak temperatures reached 7.8°C and 14.9°C,
respectively. When the supplemental heating load was increased to 90kW, the maximum peak
temperature reached 32.2°C, and the minimum peak temperature never fell below 2.7°C, which is
slightly warmer than the undisturbed ground temperature.

After running these preliminary models, it was determined that another model would be needed to
accurately model the BTES system required for this project. While GLHEPro allows for the
simulation of supplemental heat injection, it treats this process in terms of monthly energy added
 43

to or subtracted from the system, rather than simulating the circulation of excess heat throughout
the ground loop fluid. GLHEPro does offer a hybrid GSHP system option, which is intended to
model the integration of external devices, such as solar collectors or boilers, with the ground loop.
However, this hybrid system does not affect the temperature of the surrounding ground, which is
a key objective for the BTES system in this study. Therefore, a more specialized modeling
approach was required to account for the dynamic interaction between the subsurface and heat
injection for long-term thermal storage.

5.2.2.2 Analytical Model

To forecast the long-term ground loop fluid temperatures in a cold-region GDHC and BTES
system over a 25-year period, analytical modeling based on the Superposition Borehole Model
(SBM) was developed (Eskilson, 1986). Waste heat from the Fort Wainwright coal-fired power
plant was input into the system for seasonal energy storage, while the expected efficiencies of
heating, cooling, and hot water systems were factored in to determine the borehole thermal loads.
Ground thermal properties derived from the 2009 TRT at Fort Wainwright (GRTI, 2009) were
used as design inputs for the GDHC and BTES modeling.

In total, six different design cases were modeled (Table 5-6). Three of these cases (design cases
1–3) considered both the small and large land areas (Figure 5-10), with the ground being charged
for five years using excess heat from the coal-fired power plant. Design cases 4-6 assumed
continuous charging of the ground throughout the entire 25-year lifespan of the BTES system.
Each design case incorporated variations in total annual heat injection, and injection years (Table
5-6). The heating, cooling, and hot water demands for the studied buildings were obtained from
EnergyPlus building energy simulations (Section 5.2.1). The model then calculated the total
borehole length required to meet these energy demands, which was subsequently divided into an
appropriate number of wells with specific depths to satisfy the design constraints of the borefield
area.

 Table 5-6. GDHC design cases.

 Supplemental Annual Injected
 Time of Total Heat Injected (% of

      1             1-5             summer                     300%                          876
      2             1-5             summer                     500%                         1,460
      3             1-5             summer                    1000%                         2,920
      4             1-25            summer                     100%                          292
      5             1-25            summer                     200%                          584
      6             1-25            summer                     300%                          876

 44

Design Inputs to GDHC BTES Simulations

The borehole thermal loads were determined based on the building heating and cooling loads,
along with a prescribed seasonal performance factor (SPF) of 4 for the heat pump in heat extraction
mode. For heat injection, an SPF of 100 was assumed, indicating direct heat transfer. The assumed
SPFs of 4 (extraction) and 100 (injection) are consistent with values observed in monitoring studies
of operating GSHP systems, where heating performance typically ranges between 3.5–5 and heat
rejection is effectively measured as direct ground injection with negligible losses (Gehlin et al.,
2021). The resulting borehole thermal loads are shown in Figure 5-11 and are called the base case.
The base case does not include any addition of supplemental heat. These thermal loads were then
analyzed under various scenarios to assess the potential for heat storage in the BTES system.

 Figure 5-11. Base case borehole thermal loads.

The model parameters were determined from the 2009 TRT (GRTI, 2009) conducted at a nearby
location (Section 4.3). The undisturbed ground temperature was set to 2.5°C, and the ground
thermal conductivity was 2.68W/m-K, as derived from the 2009 TRT. The TRT data was
reanalyzed using the line source method with parameter estimation (Spitler et al., 2016) to
determine the borehole thermal properties. The thermal resistance used in the analytical model was
0.145m-K/W.

Simulations were performed to ensure that the minimum fluid temperature did not fall below
-10°C. Given that ground temperatures in the region are already close to freezing, heat extraction
with GSHP could lower the ground loop fluid temperature below 0°C. Glycol can be added to the
ground loop to prevent freezing. Additionally, simulations were conducted to ensure that the BTES
system could be effectively implemented on two potential drilling sites (Figure 5-10).

System Design

The system design simulations were carried out in two phases. The first phase included design
cases 1 through 3, which involved adding substantial heat to the inlet fluid of the BHE during the
summer months of the first five years of operation. After the fifth year, no additional heat was

 45

added to the fluid pumped into the BHE, aside from the heat extracted from cooling the buildings.
For this phase, separate sets of BTES sizing simulations were performed to fit the two land plots.
This was necessary because the BTES systems at the larger land plot were expected to have shorter
in overall length of pipe, due to the greater ground volume available through increased spacing
between boreholes.

The second phase included design cases 4 through 6, in which heat is injected into the BTES
system during summers throughout its entire lifetime. The seasonal heat injection reduces the
required number of boreholes, allowing the BTES system to fit on either of the two land plots.

In design case 1, a total of 876 Megawatt hours (MWh) of heat was injected into the BTES system
during the summer months over five years, which was three times the yearly net heating demand
of the base case. This injection corresponds to the total heat produced by the coal-fired power plant
each summer. After five years, the heat injection was solely due to meeting building cooling loads
(Figure 5-11). Figure 5-12 shows the corresponding borehole thermal loads for design case 1. The
remaining heat extraction from the BTES system aligned with the base case heating loads
throughout the simulation period. The simulation results indicate that a BTES system comprising
72 boreholes, each 87m deep, arranged in a 4 x 18 rectangular configuration with 11m spacing,
will meet the design requirements. This borefield size fits the small land plot identified for the
BTES system installation (Figure 5-10). The total borehole system length for this design is 6,264m
(Table 5-7).

minimum and maximum simulated fluid temperatures exiting the BTES system are -9.2°C and
25.6°C, respectively. The maximum exit temperature occurs in the summer of the fifth year, while
the minimum exit temperature is reached in the winter of the twenty-fifth year. After five years,
the borehole fluid temperatures decrease once heat injection from the power plant ceased.

 (small area).

For design case 1, a larger borefield area was also modeled (Location 2). The additional area
allowed for the possibility of a BTES system consisting of 64 boreholes, each 83m deep, arranged
in an 8 x 8 square configuration with 20m spacing. This borehole layout meets the design
requirements for heating loads and the available area for boreholes. The total borehole system
depth for this case is 5,312m. Figure 5-14 presents the simulated fluid temperatures entering and
leaving the BTES system. The minimum and maximum simulated fluid temperatures exiting the
system are -9.2°C and 27.5°C, respectively. As in the previous case, the maximum exit temperature
occurs in the summer of the fifth year, while the minimum exit temperature occurs in the winter
of the twenty-fifth year. As before, the borehole fluid temperatures decrease after heat injection
from the power plant ceases at the end of the fifth year.

 47

 Figure 5-14. BTES entering (left) and leaving (right) fluid temperatures for design case 1
 (large area).

In design case 2, the total heat injected into the BTES system during the first five summers was
five times the yearly net heating demand of the base case. This means that 1,460MWh of heat
produced by the power plant would need to be injected into the ground annually for the first five
years. After five years, the heat injections match the base case cooling loads. Heat extraction from
the BTES system corresponded to the base case heating loads throughout the simulation period.
The borehole thermal loads for design case 2 are shown in Figure 5-15.

 Figure 5-15. Borehole thermal loads for design case 2.

The simulation results for design case 2 suggest that a BTES system comprising 64 boreholes,
each 89 m deep, arranged in a 4 x 16 rectangular configuration with 12m spacing, will meet the
design requirements and fit the small land plot identified for the installation of the BTES system.
The total borehole system depth for this case is 5,696m. Figure 5-16 presents the simulated fluid
temperatures entering and leaving the BTES system. The minimum and maximum simulated fluid
temperatures exiting the BTES system are -9.2°C and 43.5°C, respectively. The maximum exit
temperature occurs in the summer of the fifth year, while the minimum exit temperature is reached
in the winter of the twenty-fifth year. Borehole fluid temperatures drop after heat injection from
the power plant ceases at the end of the fifth year.

The simulation results for design cases 1 through 3 indicate that the prescribed heat injection into
the BTES system during the first five years of operation is not an optimal solution. The resulting
borehole fields from these design cases are quite large. Additionally, during the first five years,
the extraction fluid temperatures are very high in heat injection mode and very low in heat
extraction mode. To address these issues, an additional three design cases were simulated,
considering heat injection from the power plant throughout the entire 25-year lifetime of the BTES
system.

In design cases 4 through 6, additional heat is injected in the summer throughout the lifetime of
the BTES system. For these design cases, the proposed design can fit either of the land areas
identified for the installation of the BTES system. In design case 4, the total heat injected into the
BTES during the summer was kept equal to the heat extracted from the system in the winter. This
means that 292MWh of heat produced by the power plant must be injected into the ground annually
to keep the BTES thermally balanced over the course of the year. The borehole thermal loads for
design case 4 are shown in Figure 5-21.

The simulation results suggest that a BTES system comprising 63 boreholes, each 85m deep,
arranged in a 3 x 21 rectangular configuration with 5m spacing, will meet the design requirements.
The total borehole system depth for this case is 5,355m. The proposed design can fit either of the
land areas identified for the installation of the BTES system. Figure 5-22 presents the simulated
fluid temperatures entering and leaving the BTES system. The minimum and maximum simulated
 51

fluid temperatures exiting the BTES system are -7.2°C and 13.1°C, respectively. These
temperatures remain consistent over the years, as annually balanced ground thermal loads were
used for the simulations.

5.2.2.3 Subsurface Models with GeoStudio

The final modeling effort investigated the effects of the proposed BTES system on the surrounding
subsurface, particularly in the presence of groundwater flow. Given the computational limitations
of extending the COMSOL Multiphysics model (Oh et al., 2025) to larger domains, a simplified
approach was taken using GeoStudio, a finite element software suite. GeoStudio allows for the
coupling of multiple analyses, such as groundwater flow and subsurface thermodynamics, using
its SEEP3D and TEMP3D modules, respectively. Previous studies, such as those by Darrow
(2010) and Garber-Slaght et al. (2023), have successfully used GeoStudio for subsurface modeling
in Alaskan applications, including those involving frozen ground and permafrost.

One key advantage of GeoStudio is its ability to simulate borehole heat injection and extraction in
a simplified manner. It uses a convective surface boundary condition, through which a convective
heat transfer coefficient and time-dependent fluid temperatures can be prescribed. This approach

 54

models the heat exchange between the ground and the BTES system by representing the borehole
as a cylindrical surface with spatially constant fluid temperatures, which allows fluid temperatures
to vary with depth. While GeoStudio simplifies subsurface heat exchange, including fluid flow
through pipes, it still provides useful insights into larger-scale system performance.

GeoStudio also offers specialized boundary conditions, such as the n-factor boundary condition,
which helps accurately calculate the ground surface temperature in areas subject to freezing or
thawing due to variable ambient air temperatures. The freezing and thawing 𝑛-factors, 𝑛 and 𝑛
respectively, are defined as the ratio of the surface freezing or thawing index to the air freezing or
thawing index (Johnston, 1981). In situations that include freezing and thawing near the surface
due to variable ambient air temperatures, the 𝑛-factor boundary condition helps to accurately
calculate the ground surface temperature for a given air temperature. For this study, air
temperatures used in the n-factor boundary condition were derived from average daily temperature
data from Fairbanks International Airport (1991 to 2020) (Figure 5-27).

A final advantage of GeoStudio is its ability to model the phase change effects of groundwater
freezing impeding subsurface flow. In the model, groundwater flow is reduced when temperatures
in saturated subsurface areas fall below freezing. Changes in pore-water pressure at 0°C are also
included in the model.

                                   between 1991-2020.

GeoStudio allows for fully coupled heat and mass transfer simulations, with forced convection of
heat via water and free convection of liquid water caused by thermally induced density variations.
To construct a model that couples subsurface heat and mass transfer, both thermodynamic and
hydrogeologic parameters must be defined. A “full thermal” material model was chosen for the
subsurface, which allows thermal properties to vary based on water saturation levels and
temperature. The hydraulic material model was represented using a saturated/unsaturated model,
allowing specification of the volumetric water content (VWC) function and hydraulic conductivity
functions, with consideration for anisotropy. A rigorous definition of each material model can be
 55

found in the GeoStudio Heat and Mass Transfer Modeling manual (Seequent Limited, The Bentley
Subsurface Company, 2022).

The model simplified the subsurface material properties by representing the entire system —
including the surrounding sediment, groundwater, casing, grout, U-bend, and the ground loop
working fluid — as a single composite. To define the material’s thermal properties, three
temperature-dependent functions are specified: thermal conductivity, volumetric heat capacity,
and the normalized unfrozen VWC.

In the GeoStudio model, the thermal conductivity versus temperature function was estimated using
2.68W/m°C for unfrozen ground temperatures as determined in the 2009 TRT and 3.62W/m°C for
frozen ground temperatures as (Kersten, 1949) measured a 35% increase in thermal conductivity
upon freezing for sandy soils in Fairbanks, Alaska. Near freezing (between 0°C and –1°C), the
GeoStudio-provided curve for sand was used as it provides a smooth transition between the frozen
and unfrozen thermal conductivity values. The volumetric heat capacity used was 2,210kJ/m 3°C
for unfrozen ground as estimated in the 2009 TRT and 1,834kJ/m 3°C for frozen ground as Haynes
et al. (1980) measured a 17% decrease in volumetric heat capacity of saturated Fairbanks silt with
freezing. The normalized unfrozen VWC function was estimated by Geostudio assuming the
normalized unfrozen VWC is that of sand. Values are summarized in Table 5-8.

 Table 5-8. Thermal properties used in GeoStudio model.

 Value Value
 Thermal Parameter
 (frozen) (unfrozen)
 Thermal conductivity (W/m°C) 3.62 2.68
                    Heat capacity (kJ/m³/°C)                 1,834           2,210

The hydraulic properties of the subsurface material were defined using a function for both the
VWC and hydraulic conductivity, each as a function of matric suction, to accommodate modeling
the shallow unsaturated zone. The open porosity of the soil was assumed to be 0.3, and the
saturated VWC value was reached when the VWC reached 0.3 (i.e., 30%). In areas with limited
soil data, GeoStudio’s estimates were used for the hydraulic material properties. For the VWC
function, GeoStudio applied its sample functions method assuming a porosity of 0.3 and a sand
sample material. Based on this, the VWC was estimated over a matric suction range of 1Pa to
1MPa, using a material compressibility of 3x10-11 1/Pa, which is a reasonable value for sand
according to Guerin et al. (2006).

For horizontal hydraulic conductivity Geostudio employs the Fredlund-Xing-Huang method
(Fredlund et al., 1994) for the defined VWC function, with a prescribed hydraulic conductivity
value of 0.001m/s when the matric suction is low (e.g., 1Pa), as per Nakanishi and Lilly (1998).
This results in a horizontal hydraulic conductivity as a function of matric suction. This model also
includes a vertical-to-horizontal hydraulic conductivity anisotropy ratio of 0.05 based on values
from Nakanishi and Lilly (1998), and accounts for a reduction in hydraulic conductivity when the
ground is frozen. These hydraulic properties are summarized in Table 5-9.
 56

 Table 5-9. Hydraulic properties used in GeoStudio model.

 Hydraulic Parameter Value
 Saturated VWC 30%
 Porosity 0.3
 Hydraulic saturated conductivity (m/s) 0.001
 Vertical/horizontal hydraulic conductivity ratio 1/200
 Soil structure compressibility (1/Pa) 3x10-11

The computational domain of this model is a 1,000m x 200m rectangle extruded to 150m depth
(Figure 5-28). The 𝑥, 𝑧, and 𝑦 axes will be referred to as east-west, north-south, and depth,
respectively. East and west correspond to maximum and minimum in 𝑥, respectively. North and
south correspond to maximum and minimum in 𝑧, respectively. The top ground surface is at zero
depth in 𝑦 while the bottom of the domain is located at -150 meters in the 𝑦 direction. The
rectangular grid of boreholes with a 5m spacing between rows and columns, is located on the east
side of the domain. The long side of the BTES (borehole array) is aligned along the east-west axis
as illustrated in Figure 5-28.

 Figure 5-28. GeoStudio model domain. BTES boreholes (blue lines) are situated at the
 upstream section of the domain.

The initial conditions for the model were established in two phases. The first phase involved
running a steady-state simulation to determine the initial subsurface temperature gradient and fluid
flow profile. In the second phase, a 730-day (2-year) transient simulation was conducted, using
ambient air temperature as the upper boundary. This was done to provide a more accurate initial
representation of the subsurface as influenced by surface temperatures and groundwater flow,
before the BTES system is introduced.

 57

For the thermal boundary conditions, the bottom of the domain was set at a constant temperature
of 2.5°C, as measured during the 2009 Thermal Response Test (TRT). This temperature represents
the undisturbed formation temperature and sets the initial subsurface temperature to 2.5°C
throughout most of the domain, (Table 4-3) except for the top few meters which were affected by
the seasonal variation in air temperature (Figure 5-27). To simulate ground freezing and thawing
processes, an 𝑛-factor boundary condition was applied at the surface. This condition adjusts the
ground temperature based on seasonal variations in air temperature, accurately modeling the
ground's response to the ambient climate.

When modeling heat injection and extraction from the BTES, a convective surface boundary
condition was applied to each borehole. A convection heat transfer coefficient was prescribed to
encompass the transfer of the heat from a flowing fluid, through the wall of the pipe, and to the
surrounding subsurface. A heat transfer coefficient of 50W/m² °C was used. The borehole
perimeter was defined as 0.44 meters. Additionally, a time-dependent function was provided for
the ground loop fluid temperature, allowing the model to simulate BTES variations in temperature
over time.

For the hydraulic boundary conditions, a head of 0.5m was applied from the eastern to the western
face of the domain, based on measurements from Fort Wainwright, as outlined in Section 4.2.
These boundary conditions, along with the specified hydraulic parameters, resulted in a subsurface
fluid flow velocity of 5 x 10⁻⁷ m/s, directed from east to west (in the negative x-direction). The
GeoStudio model simulated the migration of the thermal plume in the subsurface under the
assumption of a constant hydraulic head, meaning that no seasonal variations in hydraulic
conditions were considered. However, if time-dependent hydraulic head data were available, it
could be incorporated into the model to account for variations over time.

In GeoStudio, the computational domain was meshed primarily using hexahedral elements (Figure
5-29). Element edge lengths were up to 15 meters, except for where the mesh was more refined in
the vicinity of the boreholes to capture more detailed behavior. The mesh within the BTES, which
is a rectangular prism measuring 120 meters by 100 meters horizontally and 30 meters vertically
from the surface, was restricted to elements with edge lengths of 5 meters or smaller. This resulted
in a total of 17,544 elements in the mesh. For the numerical solver, a monthly time step was used,
meaning the model ran through a total of 300 time steps over the course of the 25-year simulation
period.

For better clarity in viewing the modeling results, temperatures along the blue line (located in the
x-direction at a depth of -45 meters) in Figure 5-30 are plotted in Figure 5-33, Figure 5-35, and
Figure 5-37. The mesh is visible in gray, with the finer resolution around the boreholes, particularly
noticeable on the right side of the plot. This setup allows for a more detailed examination of the
temperature distribution in the area surrounding the boreholes.

 58

 Figure 5-29. Mesh of modeling domain, split in half to allow a view of the mesh at the
 BTES.

Figure 5-30. Mesh used in GeoStudio model. Blue line outlined on modeling mesh indicates
 location of temperatures plotted in result figures below.

To illustrate the model results, slices of the temperature field in the subsurface are shown. A cross-
sectional view with depth is presented on the left side of Figure 5-31, while a subsurface map view
at -50 meters is shown on the right. The cross-section is positioned between lines of boreholes,
and while it does not directly intersect the boreholes, it is offset by 2.5 meters. The total distance
shown from east to west spans the entire length of the modeling domain, which is 1 kilometer.
This visualization allows for a clear representation of the temperature distribution across different
depths and locations within the subsurface.

  Figure 5-31. Slices through modeling domain which describe the location of the results,
 including cross section with depth (left), and map view at –50m depth (right).

The analytical model presented in Section 5.2.2.2 indicated that design cases 4 through 6 would
provide more favorable heat storage compared to design cases 1 through 3. Consequently, the
GeoStudio modeling effort focused on using the borehole configurations from design cases 4 to 6.
The results (Figure 5-33, Figure 5-35, Figure 5-37, and Figure 5-38) illustrate the evolution of the
temperature plume over time over the course of 25 years. For design case 6, additional detail is
provided with cross-sectional views of the modeled subsurface temperature. These cross sections
offer a clearer visualization of how the thermal plume migrates and evolves within the ground over
an extended period of time.

 Figure 5-32. Prescribed fluid temperatures in BTES over time in design case 4. The fluid
temperature was determined by averaging the inlet and outlet temperatures in Figure 5-22
 and was prescribed in the GeoStudio model through a convective surface boundary
 condition.

A 25-year transient simulation was developed for design case 4, with heat injection and extraction
occurring within the grid of boreholes. This configuration consisted of 3 rows of 21 boreholes each
spaced 5 meters apart and drilled to a depth of 85 meters. The fluid temperature used in the
GeoStudio model, shown in Figure 5-32, represents the average of the inlet and outlet fluid
temperatures as depicted in Figure 5-22. Over the 25-year simulation period, the average injection
temperature in design case 4 closely aligned with the natural groundwater temperature. As a result,
 60

the thermal impact on the surrounding subsurface was minimal and did not cause significant lasting
effects downstream (Figure 5-33).

For design case 6, a 25-year transient simulation was conducted with heat injection and extraction
occurring within the grid of boreholes. This design case included 2 rows of 20 boreholes each
spaced 5 meters apart and extending to a depth of 94 meters (Table 5-7). The fluid temperature
used in the GeoStudio model was the average of the inlet and outlet fluid temperatures, as shown

Figure 5-36. Figure 5-37 demonstrates the higher temperatures in the soil in the borehole field and
downstream over the 25-year simulation.

Figure 5-36. Fluid temperatures in BTES over time in design case 6. The fluid temperature
  was determined by averaging the inlet and outlet temperatures in Figure 5-26 and was
 prescribed in the GeoStudio model through a convective surface boundary condition.

Temperatures in the subsurface near the BTES system follow the borehole fluid temperatures
month to month. Warmer temperatures were observed in the summer months, when more heat was
injected into the subsurface. The plume of affected groundwater moved downstream with the
natural groundwater flow, as illustrated in Figure 5-38 via temperature modeled in the subsurface.

 62

 a) b)

Figure 5-37. Temperature at 45 m depth through the BTES field 2.5m away from boreholes
in the a) summer and b) winter of Year 1, 5, 10, 15, 20, and 25 of operation (design case 6).
Distance is east to west through bore field and downstream. Borefield is located from 100m
 to 200m.

In design case 4, which is based on balanced heating and cooling loads, the injection and extraction
processes do not significantly disturb the natural groundwater temperatures over time. Beyond the
BTES location, no major temperature changes were observed. However, design cases 5 and 6,
which involved a net addition of heat to the subsurface, show a marked increase in temperature.
Design cases 5 and 6, however, have a net addition of heat to the subsurface. In design case 6,
temperatures above ambient can be observed 685m downstream of the edge of the BTES after 25
years of use, with a temperature increase of 1°C seen 579m downstream and a temperature increase
of 5°C seen at 403m downstream. Groundwater closer to the BTES has higher temperatures due
to the heat injected, reaching 20°C at 50m downstream of the boreholes in design case 6 after 25
years.

In Figure 5-35 and Figure 5-37, it appears a spike in temperature is observed around 210m. This
is due to the groundwater movement during temperature changes in the borefield. A zone of
increased temperature can be seen immediately downstream of the borefield as the heated
groundwater moves downstream after the borehole is used for rejecting heat into the subsurface
and before the borehole is used for withdrawing heat. As the BTES is used for withdrawing heat,
temperatures of the groundwater within that zone (100m to 200m) are lowered.

In each design case modeled with GeoStudio (design cases 4 – 6), modeling results indicate the
warmest ground temperatures occur in the summer of the final year of the simulation. This
corresponds to the warmest ground loop temperatures, when the GSHP is removing heat from the
buildings. The highest temperatures were observed at the borehole, especially in design case 6 the
highest supplemental heat addition scenario, where the borehole edge reached a maximum
temperature of 54.6°C during the summer of the final year of the simulation due to prolonged heat
injection and high ground loop temperatures (Table 5-10). In contrast, design case 4 saw a
maximum temperature of 11.3°C, and design case 5 reached a maximum of 30.5°C. As shown in
Table 5-10 the warmer subsurface temperatures are mostly close to the borehole. Throughout all
the design cases, the lowest temperatures observed were -6.7°C and located in the top 3m of the

 63

subsurface in winter. This is due to the cooling effect of winter air temperatures. Effects of air
temperature only influence only the top few meters of the ground.

at 50m below ground level after 0, 5, 10, 15, 20, and 25 years of use of the system in design
 case 6.

5.3 DESIGN AND LAYOUT OF TECHNOLOGY COMPONENTS

Design case 4, which balances heat extraction and injection over the 25 year life of the system was
used to develop initial design documents. The 35% and 65% schematic design development for
Buildings 3001 and 3002 was assigned to RESPEC Company, LLC, a private firm. RESPEC’s
first task involved an as-built determination, which included reviewing current building data and
planning a topographical site survey. Their survey team developed preliminary piping, manifold
locations, and routing between the field and the connected buildings.

The mechanical and electrical teams at RESPEC worked collaboratively to design the connection
of field piping through a collection manifold and its routing into each building. They provided
preliminary sizing for the heat pumps and any necessary circulating equipment, along with
preliminary specifications and operational sequencing to integrate the new systems into the
existing HVAC and electrical systems of each building. Once the site and the areas surrounding
Buildings 3001 and 3002 were delineated and the existing systems were documented, RESPEC
developed a 35% schematic design.

At the 35% design stage, RESPEC analyzed site data collected during the as-built phase and
conducted calculations and modeling for the schematic piping layout to support the site’s thermal
storage. The schematic design documents delivered at this stage included:

  Design Narrative detailing the system design intent, BTES connectivity, functionality,
 and sequencing,
  Preliminary equipment sizes and identification,
  Preliminary system diagrams and layouts, and
  Preliminary specifications.

For the 65% design phase, RESPEC refined the schematic designs based on feedback from the
NLR and CRREL teams, incorporating revisions into the design development documents (see 65%
Design Narrative in Appendix B). This phase involved selecting specific equipment, updating
specifications for equipment, piping, and site work, and refining piping details and diagrams to
integrate the geothermal energy storage field into the existing building systems. The 65% design
development documents included in the 0 are:

  Revised Design Narrative reflecting comments and discussions from the 35% schematic
 design phase,
  Further developed equipment selections,
  Revised and updated system diagrams and layouts, and
  Specifications for equipment and layouts in SpecsIntact format

The deliverables from RESPEC are included in Appendix B through Appendix E.

In summary, the system will utilize an 80-ton water-to-water heat pump (derated for lower GHX
temperatures this unit will deliver approximately 68 tons of load capacity, Appendix E) employing
a 50% glycol solution for both source and load sides (50% glycol is the standard for all FWA
 65

building heat delivery systems), with a discharge temperature of 54°C and an expected source
temperature of about 4°C. A single circulating pump will distribute heated glycol to Buildings
3001 and 3002, splitting flow approximately equally between the two structures. Redundancy is
incorporated on the source side with duplex circulating pumps operating in a lead/standby
configuration to ensure continuous glycol circulation through the loop field in the event of pump
failure.

5.4 COST ESTIMATES

A rough order of magnitude estimate for installation costs has been developed based on
preliminary design information. These costs are subject to change pending detailed bidding and
final design refinements. Current estimates are as follows:

         Civil: $850,000
         Controls: $200,000
         Electrical: $61,000
         Insulation: $538,000
  General contractor work: $1,201,000
         Drilling: $2,500,000
  Contaminant sampling: unknown at this time

It is important to note that while a detailed cost estimate is crucial for comparison and future
feasibility studies, our request for proposals yielded only a single response despite reaching out to
several contractors. As this was the sole bid, it limited our ability to perform a comparative analysis
or secure a more granular cost breakdown. This challenge is a key consideration for future
procurement efforts.

Furthermore, significant cost elements remain to be fully quantified. The drilling costs is estimated
to be $2.5 million, based on an estimate of $420 per meter depth from a similar project in Alaska.
The cost of contaminant sampling, which is necessary on military land in Alaska, is currently
unknown and will require further site investigation to determine scope and associated expenses.

 66

6.0 FINAL RESULTS
Fort Wainwright in Fairbanks, Alaska, experiences a sub-Arctic climate with a mean annual
temperature of -2.1°C and significant seasonal variation. The installation currently relies on a high-
temperature steam-to-glycol district heating system. Given this context, Buildings 3001 and 3002
– which utilize in-floor radiant heat and are near potential BTES installation areas – present an
opportunity to explore alternative heating solutions. To that end, computer simulations explored
integrating a GSHP and BTES system to create a GDHC network, while recognizing the site’s
extreme heating-dominated load. Simulations demonstrate a GDHC with BTES system can be
designed to satisfy building heating and cooling demands while remaining within the footprint of
either Location 1 (220 meters by 40 meters) or Location 2 (250 meters by 200 meters).

The primary benefit of this proposed system is the substantial operational energy savings it would
generate. The analysis projects a 36% annual heating energy reduction for Building 3002 and a
55% reduction for Building 3001, as detailed in Figure 5-9. These impressive savings are a direct
result of the system's high efficiency, achieving a CoP of 3. It is important to note that this analysis
focuses specifically on the building zones compatible with low-temperature heat delivery.

Designing a sustainable GDHC and BTES system in a heating-dominated climate presents a
unique challenge. One solution involves injecting additional heat into the BTES system. While
this can be achieved in several ways, two scenarios were evaluated here: injecting heat only during
the summer of the first 5 years of operation, or continuously throughout the entire 25-year lifetime
of the system.

Resulting borehole configurations from analytical modeling require between 40 and 72 boreholes,
where depths ranged from 81 to 94 meters. Borehole spacing varied between 5 and 20 meters to
optimize thermal performance and accommodate site constraints. These parameters represent the
range of values explored during simulations to achieve optimal system performance and fit within
the available installation locations.

Simulations showed that injecting heat into the BTES system only during the initial five years of
operation proved unsustainable, requiring excessively large borehole fields and creating
temperature imbalances. The high temperatures generated in the ground loop during the summer
months, when cooling was needed for the buildings, created challenges in managing the system.
Continuously injecting supplemental heat throughout the 25-year system lifespan resulted in a
more viable design with reasonable ground loop temperatures and allowed the BTES to fit within
either of two potential on-site installation locations selected. Continuous heat injection also
reduced the overall amount of supplemental heat needed annually.

The subsurface model results provided valuable insights into the location and magnitude of heat
stored in the subsurface surrounding the BTES system. As the boreholes cycle through heating and
cooling operations to serve the buildings’ energy demands, the surrounding groundwater responds
by heating and cooling. When heating and cooling loads are balanced, as in design case 4, no
significant effects on surrounding groundwater temperatures were observed. However, in cases
 67

where large amounts of heat were injected, such as in design cases 5 and 6, the groundwater
temperature increased significantly and was observable far downstream. The highest groundwater
temperatures were found directly within the BTES area, particularly during the summer months,
when heat injection was at its peak. This oversized GHX creates a thermal plume that drifts
downstream, posing significant environmental and geotechnical risks.

If the plume interacts with surface water, the warmer temperatures can reduce dissolved oxygen,
harming fish populations and promoting harmful algal blooms. The consequences are even more
severe in permafrost regions, where the introduction of warm groundwater can thaw the frozen
soil. This leads to ground subsidence, destabilizing critical infrastructure such as building
foundations, roads, and pipelines if built on permafrost. Therefore, understanding the path and
temperature of this plume is critical for long-term risk management if these high supplemental heat
rates are pursued.

In summary, the results underscore the importance of creating a balanced system. This balance is
achieved when the energy extracted from the ground is replenished with an equivalent amount of
supplemental heat—in this case, from the CHP plant. Achieving this equilibrium is critical for a
sustainable and manageable design. As the findings showed, injecting too little energy would cause
the system's performance to degrade over time. Conversely, adding excess energy had the potential
to create a thermal plume capable of thawing permafrost downstream of the ground heat
exchanger.

The analysis identified Case Study 4, with its 3 x 21 borehole field, as the best-balanced system
for this installation, ensuring both long-term performance and environmental safety.
Consequently, 65% design documents were developed based on this design case. Preliminary
installation cost estimates total approximately $2.85 million, encompassing civil, controls,
electrical, insulation, general contractor work, excluding drilling. However, this estimate should
be considered a rough order of magnitude. The final project budget remains uncertain due to
unquantified costs for drilling (currently estimated as $2.5 millions) and contaminant sampling.
This uncertainty is compounded by the fact that the estimate was derived from a single contractor
bid, as multiple requests for proposals yielded only one response. This limited our ability to
perform a comparative analysis and secure a more granular cost breakdown, and therefore all costs
are subject to change pending detailed bidding and final design refinements.

The implementation of BTES systems, despite offering significant energy efficiency gains, carries
inherent risks. These include uncertainties related to subsurface geological conditions requiring
detailed site investigations, potentially high drilling costs and unforeseen subsurface obstructions,
and the long-term maintenance of borehole integrity to prevent thermal losses. Successful
integration with existing HVAC systems and adherence to geothermal-specific permitting and
regulatory requirements are also critical. Proactive risk mitigation through thorough planning,
robust system monitoring, and adherence to best practices is essential for project success.

 i

 65% Design

 1.0 INTRODUCTION
 In order to study Borehole Thermal Energy Storage (BTES) in the arctic, the National Renewable Energy
 Laboratory (NREL) and Cold Regions Research and Engineering Laboratory (CRREL) reached out to
 RESPEC to design a heat pump system. NREL has selected two buildings on Ft. Wainwright as the test
 subjects for such a system. The two buildings are 3001and 3002 and were selected due to each having a
 low temperature glycol heating system.

 2.0 GENERAL ARRANGEMENT
 A water-to-water heat pump and associated equipment will be installed in Building 3001. Piping will be
 routed from 3001 to 3002 such that the heat pump will be able to supply heat to both buildings. A second
 set of pipes will be routed from 3001 south to a utilidor that runs east-west along Montgomery Rd. Once
 inside the utilidor the pipes will run east until they enter a vault at the corner of Montgomery Rd and
 Santiago Ave.
 The pipes will turn inside the vault and exit south inside a utilidor that parallels Santiago Ave. Once the
 pipes have crossed Montgomery Rd the pipes will exit the utilidor to the west into a field that is the
 proposed location of the BTES loop field.

 3.0 PRIMARY GOALS AND OBJECTIVES
 The primary goal is to develop a design for the BTES heat pump system that will allow NREL and CRREL to
 solicit cost estimates for construction. The design is intended to integrate the heat pump system with the
 existing building heating systems and the bore hole field. The schematic drawings and discipline
 narratives have been developed to allow NREL and CCREL to review and provide input.

  2

RSI-I1911.24001 is available at no cost from the National Laboratory of the Rockies at www.nlr.gov/publications.
 65% Design

 4.0 CIVIL DESIGN
 4.1 DESIGN REFERENCES
 / Army Installation Design Guide (IDG), Fort Wainwright, Alaska, November 2006
 / Unified Facilities Criteria (UFC) 3-201-01 Civil Engineering
 / UFC 3-210-05FA, Design: Landscape Design and Planting Criteria
 / United Facilities Criteria (UFC) 3-250-01FA, Design: Pavement Design for Roads, Streets,
 Walks, and Open Storage Areas
 / Doyon Utilities Design and Construction Standards

 4.2 SITE LOCATION
 This project is located between Buildings 3001 and 3002 along Montgomery Road near the intersection
 with Santiago Avenue at Ft. Wainwright in the Fairbanks North Star Borough of Alaska.

 Figure 1: Project Location

 4.3 PROPERTY
 4.3.1 USE AND ENCUMBRANCES
  3 Buildings 3001 and 3002 house the 52nd General Support Aviation Battalion Headquarters and
 classrooms. The proposed geothermal lines are meant to be used for heating and cooling. The
 geothermal lines will need to be placed under the sidewalks between the two buildings, then run parallel

 65% Design

 to Mongomery Road through the existing DOYON-owned utilidor to the intersection of Santiago
 Avenue, exiting the utilidor into the proposed location of the boreholes in the BTES loop field.

 4.4 EARTHWORK
 Earthwork for the facility would consist of the demolition of the existing sidewalks, trenching of soils to
 install geothermal lines, and excavation of soils for new and existing utility vaults. Pipes will be bedded
 on all sides. Trenches will be backfilled with existing materials to a prepared subgrade, free of organic
 and deleterious materials, overlain by classified fill materials to establish the finished grade and a
 replacement of the sidewalks. At grassed areas, the typical section would likely consist of subbase,
 overlain by topsoil and seed.

 4.5 LAYOUT
 The 4” geothermal piping will follow the path as shown on the drawing sheets approximately 10 feet
 away from the buildings, 6 feet below finish grade, at a constant slope, avoiding other utilities by a
 minimum of 4 feet, which may include communication, electric and fire. This path should reduce the
 anticipated utility conflicts as well as minimize the amount of concrete sidewalk that needs to be
 removed and replaced. Any utilities that may be impacted by the trenching will be protected in place.
 Placing the piping in the existing utilidor will extend the length of piping needed but reduce the amount
 of earthwork excavation and utility conflicts.

 4.6 VAULTS
 Watertight concrete utility vaults compliant with DOYON Utilities standards shall be provided for
 connections where the geothermal lines enter and exit the DOYON-owned utilidor that runs along
 Montgomery Road and Santiago Avenue. The vaults will be large enough for access to piping, with a
 minimum 36” x 42” opening, and with the steel piping entering at a depth of 6 feet below finish grade.
 Per ASTM C891, vaults must be placed on evenly compacted bedding with a thickness of 4 to 6 inches.
 Bedding should be free of large rocks or any obstructions that may put a point load on the base since
 cracking may occur once a load is applied.

  4

 65% Design

 5.0 MECHANICAL DESIGN
 5.1 DESIGN CRITERIA
 / UFC 1-200-01 DoD Building Code
 / UFC 3-401-01 Mechanical Engineering
 / UFC 3-410-01 Heating, Ventilating and Air Conditioning Systems
 / UFC 3-410-02 Direct Digital Control for HVAC and Other Building Control Systems
 / UFC 3-440-01 Noise and Vibration Control
 / Doyon Utilities Design and Construction Standards
 / NREL Loop Field Design Capacity of 50 tons

 5.2 PIPING MATERIAL
 Piping larger than 3 inches shall be ASTM A53 schedule 40 steel with ASME B36.10 welded joints.
 Fittings shall be Carbon Steel Long radius ASTM A105 with butt weld ends. Flanged connections shall
 be forged carbon steel ASTM A105 weld neck.
 Piping smaller than 3 inches shall be Type L copper with brazed fittings. Dielectric nipples shall be used
 between dissimilar metals.
 The piping shall be insulated, indoor piping will receive 2 inches of fiberglass insulation and outdoor
 piping shall receive 3 inches of closed cell foam insulation.
 Valves larger than 3 inches shall be flanged gate or globe style, valves smaller than 3 inches shall be
 brazed or threaded ball or globe style.

 5.3 HEAT PUMP SYSTEM
 5.3.1 HEAT PUMP
 The heat pump shall be a water-to-water heat pump with a nominal load side heating capacity of 80
 tons. A load side 80 ton heating capacity has been selected to match the loop field (source) design
 capacity. A 50 percent glycol heating solution (based on volume) shall be used as the heat transfer fluid
 on both the load and source side of the heat pump. The load discharge glycol temperature of the heat
 pump shall be 130°F with an estimated incoming source glycol temperature of 40°F.

 5.3.2 DISTRIBUTION
 A single circulating pump shall be provided for the load side of the heat pump and will distribute glycol
 to the low temperature glycol systems in Buildings 3001 and 3002. Approximately half of the flow will go
 to each building, with the supply to 3001 matching the flow needed for the low temperature glycol in
 that building.
 Duplex circulating pumps shall be provided for the source side of the heat pump. The duplex pumps will
 operate in a lead/standby arrangement such that if the lead pump should fail the standby pump would
  5 come online and ensure glycol circulation through the loop field.

 65% Design

 5.4 HEATING SYSTEM ACCESSORIES
 Additional heating plant components include an inline air separator, diaphragm type expansion tank,
 and a packaged glycol make-up tank.

  6

 65% Design

 6.0 ELECTRICAL DESIGN
 6.1 DESIGN CRITERIA
 / UFC 1-200-01 DoD Building Code
 / UFC 3-501-01 Electrical Engineering
 / 2023 NFPA 70 National Electric Code
 / Doyon Utilities Design and Construction Standards

 6.2 UTILITY POWER AND ELECTRICAL SERVICE
 Electric power is provided to building 3002 from a 300 kVA pad-mounted transformer located
 between the buildings, and to building 3001 from a 112.5 kVA pad-mounted transformer between
 the buildings and Montgomery Road. These transformers are in turn served from a 4-way
 sectionalizer co-located with the building 3001 transformer. This sectionalizer also serves a
 transformer for a dedicated HBO service in the parking lot east of building 3001, and according to
 the available record drawings has one open space.

 The electric power required for the mechanical equipment selected is greater than the available
 capacity on either of the existing service transformers, so a new transformer will be required. We
 propose to install a new pad-mounted transformer, sized by the utility but expected to be 112.5 or
 150 kVA, next to the existing building 3002 transformer and fed from the currently-open space in
 the sectionalizer. This transformer will then feed a new service mounted on building 3001 and
 dedicated to the heat pump equipment. Individual loads will be served by a new 480V 225A three-
 phase I-Line-type distribution panel mounted in the equipment cage. There should be no new
 electric power requirements at building 3002 nor at the borehole field.

 6.3 EQUIPMENT POWER
 6.3.1 HEAT PUMP
 The heat pump has a listed full-load power requirement of 135A with a minimum circuit ampacity of
 152.9A. It will be fed from a circuit breaker on the new distribution panel.

 6.3.2 CIRCULATING PUMPS
 The three circulating pumps will be powered from individual circuits in the new distribution panel.
 The two 7.5 hp source-side pumps will be non-coincidental with each other.

 6.3.3 GLYCOL MIXING TANK
 We propose adding a new 120 volt 15A receptacle inside the equipment cage to power the cord-
 and-plug glycol mixing tank (GMT): this receptacle would be added to the existing receptacle circuit
  7
 in the room, powered from existing branch panel P-1, and would be the only equipment not powered
 from the new distribution panel. This avoids having a small transformer simply to power only the

 65% Design

 glycol tank. If it were deemed important to keep this tank on the new electrical service with the
 other project-related equipment, it should be a fairly simple matter to add a small wall-mounted
                277:120V transformer.

 6.4 ANCILLARY TASKS AND MODIFICATIONS TO EXISTING INSTALLATIONS
 One existing duplex receptacle will be relocated outside of the equipment cage to minimize the loss
 of receptacles to the larger room (one existing receptacle and the new GMT receptacle will be left
 inside the equipment cage).

 Existing luminaires will be left in place unless they conflict with the equipment cage, in which case
 they will be relocated or reoriented as required; existing lighting controls will also remain. We are
 considering whether it will be necessary or advantageous to add some lighting inside the cage and
 controlled by a switch inside the cage: this will be informed by the illumination we expect within the
 cage and whether the occupancy sensors in the room can reliably activate on occupancy inside the
 cage. If illumination is adequate, we could also change the control scheme for only the lighting
 inside the cage, and leave the luminaires themselves in place. We are also considering whether we
 will need to add emergency lighting within the cage; emergency lighting in the room is currently
 provided by two troffers across the room on an emergency circuit, and we will have to determine if
 this arrangement provides sufficient egress light for a workman inside the cage. If necessary,
 resolving this could be as simple as adding an emergency light unit inside the cage, or adding one of
 the troffer luminaires in the cage to the emergency circuit.

 At this stage, we do not expect any data/telecom requirements or changes to the fire alarm/security
 systems in the building.
